These were brought to light this past year through the awakening and diligence of the Lord of *Plessis* <note>Philippe de Mornay (1549–1623), Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent French Huguenot writer often called the "Huguenot Pope."</note>, the fierce opponent and fighter of the Antichrist in France.

And although many things are pointed out in these *Indices* <term>Index (plural Indices): A list of books forbidden by the Catholic Church</term> or pointers as evil or contemptible, nevertheless, not all those who dispute or disagree with the Pope's opinion are expressed therein, as we can well observe.

<column-break/>

I believe that these many-eyed *Argus* <note>In Greek mythology, Argus Panoptes was a giant with a hundred eyes; here used to describe the watchful censors.</note> and Guardians of the Monster of the Inquisition also saw those [passages] which were left out; but they passed them by either deliberately or willfully, so that they would not seem to have entirely rejected so great a man as *Erasmus*, whom they (as has been said) count in some way as one of their own.

The Jesuits today, being the most cunning breed of monks, who claim all glory for themselves, strive to erase and pluck *Erasmus* from human memory as being more wicked and harmful than *Luther* and *Calvin*. These Sophists and Hypocrites have their Jesuitical reasons for this. Therefore, the more they declare the same *Erasmus* to be rejectable, the more earnestly we maintain that he must be read.

# MARTIN
## LUTHER.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial W featuring scrolling foliate patterns and floral motifs.</image-desc>
Who shall be able to praise or extol you worthily enough, great **LUTHER** (who in the beginning preached the Gospel with such steadfastness)? You, who—when the Christian, or rather the Roman **WORLD**, lay smothered and sunken in the deepest and thickest darkness—then enlightened the same through the torch of the Gospel, and ignited the hearts that were nearly perished in love and entirely grown cold. You have truly, with greater joy and exceptional courage, revealed the Roman Idolatry to the Christian world, more so than those who, near that same time, sent word of the New World to *Spain* <note>A reference to the discovery of the Americas, contrasting the physical New World with Luther's spiritual revelation</note>. God raised him up when He, according to His great mercy, had intended to use this chosen Doctor or teacher to spread the Gospel of Christ His son: exactly at that time when the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Pope</note> was raging most fiercely and exercising the extreme tyranny over the Church of God. Also when the art of money-making, of gold and silver, was most highly esteemed and in full swing, but Christ’s blood (against which nothing of value can be compared) was disregarded and held as a trifle; When the *Dispensations* <note>Exemptions from church law</note> and the *Markets of Indulgences* forgave all sins and villainies, or permitted them to go unpunished, and Heaven (through the Pope’s full power, as they say) was promised and firmly pledged to those who gave the most money for it; *Luther*, then exercising the office of Preacher in Saxony, very quietly and modestly, like one who still doubts and wishes to further probe and know the truth, undertook the first disputation concerning the *Indulgences* (which were being preached and announced everywhere with such diligence and labor). The *Theses* or articles of which, against the *Indulgences* and their salesman or market-crier *Tetzel*, were accepted with great pleasure by many learned men devoted to the Church as well as the Fatherland: Among whom was also the aforementioned *Erasmus of Rotterdam*, who at that time (oh, had he always done so!) greeted *Luther* through his letters and confirmed him in this: As did also *Albertus Krantz* (who [wrote] of the Popes...

# MARTIN

<note>original: "MARTINVS"</note>

steadfast cunning and practices noted in fine histories) also accepted the same as good: but nevertheless worrying for *Luther* because of the power and arch-deceit of the Pope. And Doctor *Stupicius* <note>Johann von Staupitz (c. 1460–1524), Luther's superior in the Augustinian order</note> who (through the urging and advice of the Elector of Saxony, as well as the entire Augustinian order and the Academy) had made *Luther* a Doctor, said to *Luther* (who was reluctant to accept such honor) while laughing:

> *It already appears as if God intends to undertake wonderful things and dealings in Heaven and earth, wherein He seems to want to use the labor of learned men; therefore God shall also put your service to work.*

And the Emperor *Maximilian* <note>Maximilian I (1459–1519), Holy Roman Emperor</note> when he had read *Luther's* Disputations against this money-hunting mule-driver and indulgence-seller *Teccelius* <note>Johann Tetzel (1465–1519), the Dominican friar famous for selling indulgences</note>, charged Doctor *Pfeffinger*, the Duke of Saxony's legate, to deliver this command: That they should take this Monk into protection, for they would before long have need of his kind against the injuries and the bold persistence of the Romans. But *Luther* daily rose to greater things: who, when he had blown into the wind and annihilated those indulgences and Bulls <note>official papal edicts</note> like eye-pleasing and quickly fading bubbles through his exceedingly great spirit, he approached the "Bogeyman" of these indulgences and the Father of all swindling, the Pope himself. He fearlessly attacked the triple-crowned head, and so expressed, painted, and described—both with tongue and pen—this *Capitoline* <note>original: "Campidolischen," referring to the Capitoline Hill in Rome</note> raging *Cerberus* <note>the three-headed hound of Hades; here a metaphor for the Pope</note>, his ugly, godless, horrible barking and powerless yelping: calling him now the *Antichrist*, and then the *Babylonian Dragon*, so that through his thundering voice and powerful writings Rome and all Italy were moved and terrified, and all the world wondered at *Luther's* teaching and its happy growth and increase: also so much that his name and fame reached to the ends of the earth. Oh, the great *Luther's* exceptional steadfastness and bravery, where you (as you traveled to the Diet at *Worms*, although your friends warned you and your enemies laid cunning snares for you) did not allow yourself to be frightened by *Huss's* <note>Jan Hus (c. 1369–1415), a reformer burned at the stake despite a promise of safe conduct</note> footsteps nor turn back. And when you departed from the assembly at *Smalcald* <note>Schmalkalden</note>, and took leave of your beloved ones, you used these very words:

> *God fill you with His blessing, and with hatred against the Pope; and again wishing others all happiness; That they, above all, should learn to know the Pope.*

And if God granted that the same...

# LUTHER.

the same cunning, fox-like nature and evil artifice, which those who today vainly boast of your name, O Luther, did not see or notice. This man of God foresaw many things which we know to have happened or occurred since; among others, he spoke thus to Doctor Schnepfius <note>Johannes Schnepf (1495–1558), a German theologian and reformer</note>:
> *After my death, few among these theologians of ours will remain steadfast: and what the Turk and the Pope do not bring about, we shall have to expect from our own people.*

And truly, godly and prophetic Luther, this has followed accordingly. For we now see many there fighting for Luther, yet (under such a cover) actually fighting against him, and overturning—indeed, almost uprooting—the principal foundations laid by him. This is done through a bitter hatred they hold against those who support the Reformed Religion and the true Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, 1530</note>, and who oppose the <term>Ubiquity</term> <note>original: "Over-al-wesen"; the Lutheran doctrine that Christ's body is present everywhere</note> of Christ’s body as an idolatrous point (first brought into being by Brentius <note>Johannes Brenz</note>, and filed and polished by Smedelinus <note>Jakob Andreae, nicknamed 'Smidelin' or 'Little Smith'</note> like a clever smith). Meanwhile, the Papists laugh at this and hope for their own advantage against them. Luther also said:
> *If our Emperor Charles undertakes anything to the detriment of the Gospel, it is certain that he shall lose his entire government in the Netherlands.*

In this, he was not very far astray, for his son Philip <note>Philip II of Spain</note> has not only undertaken actions against Religion, but also, according to Spanish counsel and practices, has endeavored with all might and force of arms to uproot and ruin the entire Netherlands. Because of this, the same Netherlands (after they had met him in everything, and were oppressed by unbearable burdens and violence) were finally forced to set themselves against this physical Tyrant—who, under the cover of Religion, rose up through his bottomless desire for domination and sought to ruin and nullify the country's Privileges and Justice. Following the courageous nature of their pious ancestors, they stood up to maintain the freedom of the Fatherland with equal power, and consequently declared him an enemy of the Republic. With which Spanish nation the United Netherlands have now been waging war for so many years, and ever more steadfastly and harder: for their own good and that of all Christian Princes, indeed the whole world. For if these Netherlands were brought low, then that would happen to them which they may now rightly foresee and sense.

# MARTIN LUTHER

**MARTIN LUTHER** wrote a great deal in the Latin and High German languages, in various places and at various times; these were first printed separately, and afterwards collected and published in several *Tomos* <note>Volumes</note> or large parts. Beyond these, an *Elenchus* <note>original: "Elenchus", meaning a cross-referenced index</note> or a *Register of all Luther’s books* was published by *Sigismund Suero*, in which it is indicated in what volume and page of each edition his writings—both German and Latin—may be found.

All of Luther's writings, which are many, we shall not enumerate here (for the sake of brevity): but we shall only set down those which were printed specifically against the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term used by Luther and his followers to refer to the Papacy</note> and his helpers; and that according to the order of time.

Propositions on *Penance* and *Indulgences*. <note>Referring to the 95 Theses of 1517</note>

*Giovanni Annio of Viterbo* <note>original: "Ioannis Annij Witerbiensis"</note> on the Pope’s *Monarchy or Empire*, a Disputation written in the *Year 1481* with the preface by *Luther*, and the Letter to the Reader against *Jacob van Hoogstraten* <note>A prominent Dominican inquisitor and opponent of the Reformation</note>.

A letter to *Sylvester Cajetan* and others.

Notes by *Luther* against the erasures <note>or "scribblings"</note> of *Eck*. <note>Johann Eck, one of Luther's primary theological adversaries</note>

Against the *Scholastics*. <note>The medieval schoolmen whose philosophy Luther rejected</note>

Defense against *Eck’s* fal-

-se accusations.

A letter to *J. Eck*, regarding the purgation of *Eck*.

Confirmation of the Articles of *Luther* rejected by the last Bull of *Leo* the tenth. <note>Referring to the Papal Bull "Exsurge Domine" issued by Pope Leo X</note>

The entire Bible translated from the He-

# No one becomes the Greatest all at once.

TECCELIUS <note>Johann Tetzel (c. 1465–1519), the Dominican friar famous for selling indulgences.</note>, boasting of the Pope’s Bulls <note>Official papal edicts, named for the 'bulla' or lead seal attached to them.</note> with all his might,
LUTHER, standing still as if amazed,
Grips the Bull-seller; thereafter, the Bull-maker’s power
He frustrates—yes, he soon overthrows the entire Bull-stall <note>A metaphor comparing the sale of indulgences to a merchant's market stall.</note>.

## Furthermore, the Papists say that the
Bulls are not to be lightly esteemed.

*Who dares to say the Pope’s Bulls are trivial things,*
*Since he carries on such a great struggle for the sake of the Bulls*
*That what he cannot achieve through war, this Bubble-Bull <note>A pun on 'Bulla' (seal/edict) and 'bubble' (something hollow or inflated).</note> accomplishes for him;*
*Why then do you call it light, that which stirs up everything far and wide?*

# PHILIP
## MELANCHTHON.

-><-

It is very fitting that here, alongside LUTHER, comes his PHILIP MELANCHTHON, who, having lit the Evangelical Torch from *Luther*, provided it with nourishment; and having orderly gathered its material and fuel together, moved it to shine more clearly and flame higher. *Luther* could not have had a more faithful nor more steadfast companion for spreading the light of the Gospel. *Luther* was vehement, *Melanchthon* was mild-mannered: *Luther* was stout-hearted, *Melanchthon* was timid: the former excelled in voice and speech, the latter in writing and reasoning: the former was powerful in preaching, the latter in teaching within the Schools: the former despised the adversaries and Sophists <note>The term "Sophists" was often used by Reformers to describe Scholastic theologians who used complex, circular logic.</note>, while the latter disputed with them for fame and honor: the former was content with Theology as a ruler; the latter desired to adorn Theology through Philosophy (as its handmaiden). *Luther* was somewhat more severe, while *Melanchthon* was more moderate in all things. Therefore, he obtained very great praise from all who were zealous in Religion. Now, as far as the learning of *Melanchthon* is concerned, it was so great that beyond the knowledge of divine things, he was also very experienced in the *Encyclopedia* <note>original: "Encyclopædiam"; referring here to the full circle of liberal arts and sciences.</note> or the deep complexities of Philosophy. These were also brought to light and taught by him with great fruitfulness. Through the help and advancement of these arts, he explained and interpreted Theology so clearly that those learning could very easily understand and comprehend it, and those who betook themselves to Theological study were able to aptly clarify its obscurities while teaching in public. He first taught by what means Theology could be joined together with Philosophy (to which he seems, in some things, to have attributed too much); he also indicated and described a certain path and order which true Students of Theology should maintain and follow in presenting, writing, and disputing. Through these two exceedingly great men, you, *Wittenberg*—I know hardly if I wish to call you a small town—obtained at that time a greater name and reputation,

<meta>Catchword: than,</meta>

<summary>This page provides a comparative character study of Philipp Melanchthon and Martin Luther, highlighting Melanchthon's role as the scholarly, moderate "handmaiden" to Luther's fiery leadership. It emphasizes Melanchthon's integration of Philosophy and the Liberal Arts into Theological study at the University of Wittenberg.</summary>

<keywords>Philipp Melanchthon, Martin Luther, Wittenberg, Reformation, Theology, Philosophy, Education, Scholasticism, Evangelical Torch</keywords>

# PHILIP

<insert>R. R.</insert> <note>Likely a reference to a specific source or contributor, possibly "R. Rescius" or a similar contemporary scholar.</note>

...as Rome, the mistress and Queen of all cities, has been able to boast of herself. Such a gathering and residence of students, such a multitude of ministers and schoolmasters, have come forth from the single school of *Melanchthon*, that the great and wide Germany, and the surrounding Kingdoms, have had learned and godly preachers and schoolmasters in their places everywhere through the benefits and advancement of *Melanchthon*. So much so that he is not unjustly called the father of preachers and teachers. Among strangers, and even the enemies of the Religion <note>Referring to the Protestant Reformation</note>, he was gladly heard (because of his singular learning, moderation, and fitness in doing all things). By whose great esteem and reputation *Francis I, King of France* (whose honor and name shall remain in eternal memory among learned men) was moved to request *Melanchthon* through letters and his Envoy: so that he might use his advice and counsel in unifying and moderating matters of Religion. So great was the kindness of this man's spirit. That in dealing with his enemies, he even obtained praise from the *Papists* <note>A term used by Reformers for Roman Catholics</note> (even when he publicly disputed with them concerning Religion). So that not a few of *Melanchthon's* adversaries judged him more justly than some of his own disciples, who already stood ungrateful against their Master as Doctors, *Aristarchs* <note>term: Aristarchen (severe or pedantic critics, named after Aristarchus of Samothrace)</note> or heel-treaders. With what study and labor he attempted to either take away or extinguish the dispute—or rather the war—arisen over the matter of the Lord's Supper: his writings and letters sufficiently testify, contained in the Book of Epistles of *Calvin* the Theologian (with whom he lived in friendship), yet all in vain, since *Luther*, full of his own mind, seems to have wanted to tolerate no one above him. *Luther* being now deceased, a heavy war arose on the point of the Lord's Supper, but after *Melanchthon's* death, the heaviest of all: other weapons were sought, new and never before used, to advocate and maintain *Ubiquity* <note>term: Ubiquiteyt (the Lutheran doctrine that Christ's body is present everywhere, including in the bread and wine)</note> or the "being everywhere," which is nowhere but in heaven: which they put forward so adorned that they feel it must be worshipped in a certain way, and quite after the example of the *Papists*. Oh, if only that war had never arisen, or having now arisen, had never been conducted with such concord or unity (as a mother of all discord or division)! Then the Pope, who truly [is] Everywhere...

<summary>This page praises Philipp Melanchthon as the "father of teachers" whose influence spread across Europe, noting his failed attempts to mediate the bitter theological "war" over the Eucharist between Lutheran and Calvinist factions.</summary>
<keywords>Philipp Melanchthon, Martin Luther, John Calvin, Francis I of France, Lord's Supper, Ubiquity, Reformation, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legacy of the Reformation, specifically focusing on the relationship between the teachings of Luther and the moderation of Melanchthon, followed by a bibliography of Melanchthon's works.</meta>

->M E L A N C H T H O N.<-

had turned his weapons and artful deeds against us, long ago (had God so willed it) to the great benefit and profit of all Christendom, he would have been snatched and cast down from the rocky cliff of his pride. But *Luther's* <note>original: "Luteri"</note> teaching must live, which the false *Lutherans* (through the hatred and envy they bear toward the name of the *Reformed*) weaken and undo. And also, *Melanchthon's* moderation must find a place among those who shriek and cry out more with words than they awaken the people with true love and reasonableness: so that we may finally, all together, take hold of the *Antichrist* (against whom *Luther* <note>original: "Luterus"</note> fought so courageously and steadfastly) and see him brought low through God's power.

<column-break/>

Melanchthon has written books without end: printed in many places, whose writings nevertheless were published together in the year 1541, printed by *Hervagius* at *Basel* in five volumes <term>Tomes; large volumes of a multi-part work</term>, in the year 1546.

Many of his Disputations are included with the works of *Luther*.

Everything pertaining to *Philosophy*, *Philology*, *Oratory*, to Good Instruction, and all manner of Arts belonging to the *Encyclopedia* <term>Encyclopedia: here meaning the "circle of learning" or a complete education</term> or perfection, are too numerous to count here.

Greek and Latin *Epigrams* or Inscriptions, etc. There are four volumes or parts of Declamations.

Two volumes of Letters.

The Chronicle of *Carion*, enlarged and improved with many histories. <note>Johann Carion originally drafted this world history, which Melanchthon heavily revised.</note>

From the same writings, Theological Oppositions and Answers, gathered into common arrangements or places <term>Loci Communes: a method of organizing theology by key topics</term>, by *Christophorus Pezelius*, a faithful man and diligent Theologian, and *Professor* of the School of Bremen.

Oh, if only the former disciples of *Philipp Melanchthon* judged their Master with more respectful love, and judged his writings more justly! Or if those who seem to love him would at least not defile his fine, worthy writings; then they would show themselves to be grateful disciples, faithful Theologians of the Church who love peace, and true opponents of the Pope's teachings, and would be immediately recognized as such.

# JOHANNES BU-
## GENHAGEN.

Just as **Luther** was joined by **Melanchthon** in his school, so too did he have **Bugenhagen** beside him in the Church. These excellent pipes of God’s organ—sounding and making heard with a wonderful unity that which **Christ**, our origin and master of Salvation, has taught us—followed **Luther**, who was like a lute-striking Evangelical Trumpet. With a very sweet and lovely, flexible tone of voice, **Melanchthon** and **Bugenhagen** followed him, doubling those voices, through whose lovely sound they might attract and delight many more tender ears. These men, as the organs or instruments of God’s Church, have spread and poured out the sound of the heavenly Gospel through all lands. In **Bugenhagen** there was a singular kindness and modesty of nature, which the appearance of his being seems to indicate clearly enough; the nature of his spirit was estranged from all ambition. And he stood before his place and calling—preaching, watching, and guarding the **Wittenberg** flock—as a faithful shepherd for many years. Denmark also felt and enjoyed the excellent, faithful works of this Doctor in the laying of the foundation of the Christian Religion, under the guidance of the best King, **Christian III**: who, in his Kingdoms and Duchies, caused the Kingdom of Christ to be preached and promoted to the best of his ability, as befits a Christian King, and also diligently maintained it. He also restored and renewed the Academy or High School of **Copenhagen**, formerly founded by King **Frederick** <note>Frederick I of Denmark</note>, his paternal grandfather, which had then fallen into decay; and he applied and funded everything that was in any way feasible for the blessed building up of the Evangelical schools. Therefore Denmark, and in the midst of it, that noble **Copenhagen**, hold this **Pomeranus** <note>A Latin nickname for Bugenhagen, referring to his birth in Pomerania</note> as their own, always holding him in honor and remembrance as one whose scholastic labor and fatherly instructions they greatly loved and followed. Above all, this Pomeranian gained a name for himself through the commentary on the Book of **Psalms**: which he first-

# JOHANNES

first published in Dutch, and afterwards in Latin: and having revised the same several times, he brought it to light at that time with great pleasure. Certain and firm teachings and consolations are taken herein from the Prophetic and Apostolic Scriptures, which have so wonderfully strengthened and uplifted simple and dejected Christian minds. Also, his labor and diligence served to the great edification and refreshment of their souls (especially during the first building-up of that Church). We still have that excellent judgment of Luther, which he gave concerning this interpretation of the Psalms by Bugenhagen: which one may read in the first edition, addressed to Frederick, Duke of Saxony. I do not consider it strange to place it here to the praise of the same Bugenhagen. These are Luther's own words: *For so that one may compare the commentaries or interpretations of both the ancient and the new writers, and although it is not properly pleasant to judge anyone, yet I dare to say of no one whose books still remain and who has interpreted the Psalms of David: among whom this Pomeranus* <note>Pomeranus was a common nickname for Bugenhagen, referring to his birthplace in Pomerania.</note> *is worthy to be called and said to be the first true interpreter. So many personal opinions, and those mostly uncertain, have almost all others had and brought together concerning this excellent book. Here truly the certain judgment of the Spirit shall teach wonders.* This is a great praise, which, being given by a great and esteemed man, can also carry more weight.

## THE POMERANIAN.

O gray-haired Pomeranian, and your faith so mighty
Has been so true / that the pious must honor you:
Since you have spread God’s powerful word far and faithfully /

# JUSTUS JONAS

->JUSTUS JONAS.<-

Rightly has **Luther** also called **Justus Jonas** "the refined one," as he was the one who never wavered from his side during the building up of the Wittenberg Church: and who also afterwards translated several of **Luther's** commentaries and treatises into Latin. This man, having studied Law and made great progress therein, considered the pursuit and knowledge of Divine matters to be of much higher value, surpassing all that human wisdom had discovered and gathered together. Yet, his learning and experience in the Law were not useless to him, as was often seen in the office and the burden laid upon him. For when handling Church matters in Synods, assemblies, and gatherings of Princes, he brought them—by the advantage and help of those studies—to a good conclusion much more skillfully and successfully than others. And through the experience in many things which he had acquired in the handling of religious matters, he was very beneficial to the Church, so that one could hardly say what the labor of such a man—by whom almost all matters occurring in Religion were heard and seen—is able to achieve and bring about in heavy and obscure ecclesiastical affairs. **Justus** showed himself an excellent advocate and defender of the lawful marriage of Priests against **Johannes Faber**, the defender and patron of all whoremongers; and this **Faber** is like **Eck** <note>Johannes Eck, a principal Catholic adversary of Luther</note>, in whose regard that common jest of **Erasmus** still remains, which sounds thus: *The poor and lowly Luther makes many men rich.* <note>original: "Den armen en geringen Luterus maeckter vele Rijck." Erasmus was mocking how Catholic polemicists gained wealth and status by writing against the Reformation.</note> For these men requested and also received a magnificent gift from the Princes for writing and refuting **Luther**. This **Justus**, as an eyewitness and listener to the things that happened in Germany regarding religious matters and which were attended by him: also saw and heard those things which **Luther**—as a Prince and strong champion of **Christ**, being about to exchange life for death—gave of himself and spoke to his friends at Eisleben in his fatherland. So that this **Justus** is a certain and principal witness of the holy labor and faithful care which **Luther** [maintained] through all his life

<meta>catchword: and; mirror text visible through paper from previous page</meta>

<meta>This page continues the account of the death of Martin Luther and the life of his close associate, Justus Jonas. The previous page likely detailed Luther's final journey to Eisleben.</meta>

# JUSTUS JONAS.

and also brought about in his death and carried out for the Church of God. Regarding which, this man of ours (as well as two other special friends) testify that these words were spoken by him <note>referring to Martin Luther</note> not long before his death: *Pray,* he said, *to God, that he may preserve the teaching of his Gospel for us. For the Pope and the Council of Trent <note>A major ecumenical council of the Catholic Church (1545–1563) seen by Reformers as a threat to their teachings</note> intend gruesome things.* And feeling that the end of his life was now approaching, he prayed thus: *My Heavenly Father, God, and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, God of all comfort, I thank you that you have revealed to me your Son Jesus Christ, whom I have believed, whom I have preached, whom I have loved, whom I have exalted, whom the Roman Pope and his godless crowd persecute. I pray you, Lord Jesus Christ, receive my soul, etc.* Speaking these last words, in the presence of *Justus Jonas*, he commended his dying soul to God as if into a lap: who indeed saw *Luther* die with sadness, but with exceeding great joy, hearing him speak thus from the heart.

From Justus Jonas these books were published:
*Defense for the Marriage of Priests, against Johannes Faber <note>A Catholic opponent of the Reformation</note>, patron or protector of all whoremongers.*
An *Oration on the Study of Theology.*
*Annotations on the Acts of the Apostles.*

<column-break/>

*Martin Luther's booklet on the Private or Secret Mass, and the Anointing of Priests, translated by him from High German into Latin.*
*Luther's Declaration on Jonah, translated by the same.*
He also *rendered into Latin* Luther's book on the *Jews and their lies.*
*Against Wicelius <note>Georg Witzel, a theologian who sought a middle ground between Catholics and Protestants</note>.*
*On the death of Luther.*

# CASPAR CRU-
## CIGER.

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut decorative initial 'C' featuring botanical flourishes.</image-desc>CASPAR CRUCIGER, born in Leipzig, a Minister of God’s word in the Church of Wittenberg, shall rightly be given a place here: both in respect to his worthy Office, as well as his exceptional learning and his great judgment, who helped *Luther* <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the central figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> not a little in the building up of Christ’s Church. This man was highly esteemed for his great experience in languages, through which he—as if in the infancy of the first-born Church—stammered and reached out those words and manners of speaking which could readily bring her to a fit and clear expression of God’s word. Also, it was no small praise for *Cruciger* when they say that *Luther*, in translating the Bible from the Hebrew into the High German tongue, often made use of this man’s counsel and approval, and relied upon his judgment. It was truly a gift and wonder of God that in the rebirth of the Church, the holy language <note>Hebrew</note> was also, as if reborn, brought to its interpretation through *Luther* (the most learned German translator) and was read and preached by the Germans. And just as *Luther* immediately became very famous through that heavy and Divine work; so also must *Cruciger* not be withheld his praise, since *Luther* himself ascribes so much to him in this work. Furthermore, being very experienced in the Mathematical Arts: eventually leaving behind the speculations and proofs of the same, he fervently embraced and preached the Christian faith: which assures us no less of the hope of our salvation than our eyes can perceive the certain rules of those same arts. In the knowledge of natural things, as also in Medicine, he had likewise greatly increased: But to the Creator of all things, and Christ the Physician of Souls, he finally surrendered and dedicated himself entirely. Among other fine Studies for which one must give him honor, this is said in his praise: that when he was called to the Assemblies first at Worms, and thereafter at Regensburg, he had a private conversation with *Calvin* <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French theologian and reformer in Geneva.</note> concerning the Lord’s Supper, and having understood his knowledge and sentiment,

The eloquent Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a French Protestant theologian and successor to Calvin</note> in the *Life of Calvin* <note>original: "in d’leven Calvini"</note> seems to wish to interpret this to the praise of them both. The one was exceedingly dear to the other; it were to be wished that many others were as inclined to be taught themselves as they are to teach! Truly, the affairs of Christendom would stand in a better and more righteous state today: and the power and practices of the *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Papacy</note> would without doubt not be able to accomplish so much. But the Church did not enjoy the faithful service of this pious man for many years, for in the 45th year of his age, his soul was transferred to that place where the Lord Christ truly ascended with his body, and where he truly remains with that same body. He died at Wittenberg, in the Year of our Lord 1548.

# GEORGE, PRINCE
## OF ANHALT, COUNT
### OF ASCANIA.

It is unnecessary that I should rouse you, beloved viewer, to look upon this man: since he is one of the number of Princes, and as such is beheld with great desire by every man. This is the *Prince of Anhalt*, and *Count of Ascania*, godly and learned: I say an exceedingly good Prince, and an exceedingly good Shepherd <note>The term "Shepherd" here refers to his role as a spiritual leader or bishop within the church.</note>. Seldom are Princes counted among the Evangelical <note>In this context, "Evangelical" refers to the early Protestant or Lutheran movement.</note>: but even more seldom are they found to be both Evangelical and shepherds. From illustrious Princes, and from an ancient Noble house and lineage, was born *George* by the grace of God, Prince and Shepherd, whose father was *Ernest*, Prince of Anhalt, Count of *Ascania*, Lord of Bernburg and Zerbst. From the lineage of *Ascania*, highly praised in virtue, glory, and fame: this *George* has so glorified and illuminated it through his new and holy studies, that it seems nothing more could have been added to expand and adorn this Noble lineage. When he had successfully exercised himself in good letters <note>The "humanities" or classical education.</note> at *Wittenberg*, and had acquired the three principal languages <note>Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note> in a princely manner; he then devoted himself entirely to the study of Theology, and was chosen as the first provost of the churches of Magdeburg and Meissen, whom (as that which we most admire in good Princes) he did not only preside over, but also advanced. For that is a true Shepherd’s office: to so preside over his own that he may be of benefit to them: and to so stand before the flock of the Lord, that he, as an exceedingly faithful Shepherd for the same, protecting them, may be seen to stand; which Prince and Shepherd, governing the people according to the Laws, and feeding the same with the doctrine of Salvation, may be named and held as the second *Ezra* <note>original: "Esdras"; a reference to the biblical priest and scribe who restored the law to Israel.</note> of our time. To whose praise this is yet to be said, namely: that when the German churches stood in a very sober and sorrowful state, he, strengthened and confirmed by God’s spirit, nevertheless faithfully fulfilled his office, and did not desert his post: but rather the affairs of the same Christian Church, to the Princes, as

-># GEORGE, PRINCE OF ANHALT<-

He also presented and urged these matters before Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (1500–1558)</note> (who was threatening the Evangelical cause with the severest punishments). His house stood open at that time as a haven and refuge for the miserable and the fugitive: he was most outstanding in generosity and hospitality toward strangers. He bore a singular love for *Melanchthon* <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a key German Reformer and collaborator of Luther</note> and his companions in labor and danger. Because of this, a place has been assigned to *Anhalt* himself among those with whom he most dearly lived and most diligently studied. Having left behind his fine example as a Prince and shepherd of the people to other Princes, he has departed for Heaven, the dwelling place of the King of all kings and the Shepherd of all princes. Having well-taught and faithfully preached that Divine matters must be preferred over human ones, and Heavenly matters over Earthly ones, he has taken up his inheritance in that Kingdom of which there is no end. He departed from this life in the 46th year of his age, and in the year of our salvation obtained in Christ, 1553. Those subjects are to be considered exceedingly happy who obtain such a great Prince, whom they may acknowledge, honor, and follow as a shepherd and caretaker of their souls as well as their bodies.

From George, Prince of Anhalt, these writings have come down to us:

A comforting Oration on the saying of *Christ*; "No one shall pluck my Sheep out of my hand," etc. Delivered at the Merseburg provincial assembly.

A sermon delivered at Torgau at the wedding of *Augustus*, Duke of Saxony, and *Anne*, daughter of the King of Denmark.

Four Sermons on both forms of the Sacrament <note>Refers to the practice of "Utraquism," giving both bread and wine to the laity during Communion</note>, in which the objections of the adversaries are dismantled and refuted with strong arguments.

<column-break/>

His Consolation to his brother *Wolfgang*, who was ill.

Two Sermons concerning false Prophets, and the true use of the Christian Religion: In which the errors of *Sidonius*, Bishop of Merseburg, are rejected.

Sermons and various writings comprising the entire true doctrine established in the pure Church.

A Treatise on the Apostolic use of ordination maintained in the purest churches adhering to the *Augsburg Confession* <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, 1530</note>.

His own views on the articles currently in dispute.

# ERASMUS SAR-
## CERIUS.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring elaborate scrolling foliage and a stylized mask at the bottom center.</image-desc>See here is **Erasmus**, that **Sarcerius Annemontanus** <note>Annemontanus refers to his birthplace, Annaberg</note>, who, after he had excellently practiced in the liberal arts and the study of all virtues, and having now acquired human learning and arts, most ardently embraced the study and investigation of the Holy Scriptures. And as he had successfully learned the same at Wittenberg (which at that time was like an Evangelical Athens <note>A metaphor for Wittenberg as the intellectual center of the Reformation</note>, where students of the Holy Scriptures flocked from all sides and from far-flung kingdoms), he was placed among the first disciples who began to spread and sow the teachings of the Gospel everywhere through preaching and writing. He was held in that time to be a very select preacher and a most capable teaching theologian, as one who, through the service of philosophy, presented and explained God's word very capably and orderly. From this it followed that at that time the *Neophytes* <term>Neophyti: young newcomers or recent converts to the faith</term>, who through *Sarcerius* their master, considered themselves very capable and learned to preach God's word, greatly loved his fine writings. He was a very eloquent preacher, first at Leipzig, a widely renowned, pleasant, and very clean city in the land of Meissen, which listened with wonder to the very sweet and pure sermons of this same *Sarcerius*. And thereafter, in the very renowned County of Nassau, he was a shepherd and select leader. This man, under *William*, Count of Nassau—father of that great *William* <note>Referring to William the Silent, leader of the Dutch Revolt</note>—proclaimed the Gospel with exceeding diligence and faith, teaching, writing, and concerning himself with that which brought and guided the churches and their ministers onto the right way and path of the Lord. He greatly praised and confirmed the civil policy and spiritual discipline or instruction, established according to the ancient canons <term>Canons: ecclesiastical rules or laws</term>. *Sarcerius*, having now found such a godly, kind, and very virtuous Prince, namely **William** of Nassau, as a foster-father of the churches, expanded the pure word of God under him with great fruitfulness. It is for the Princes a
->great<-

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of notable figures, transitioning here to the life and legacy of Erasmus and the religious reformers associated with the House of Nassau.</meta>

-> E R A S M U S <-

It is a great praise to love godliness, and to place the same above all worthy and highly esteemed things. But as godliness is threefold, consisting in the love of God, of the fatherland, and of one's parents; we see these as inherited and innate qualities assigned and left to the noble House of Nassau. Whose godliness has appeared and been expressed above all in William, surnamed the Great <note>William the Silent (1533–1584), Prince of Orange and Count of Nassau</note>, who by means of his heroic virtues was declared: *A father of the Fatherland, a champion of liberty, and a patron and protector of religion.* Who, out of necessity, along with the other Netherlandish Princes (having first attempted and proposed everything which would not have been done by others), being compelled, has shown by deed that the fatherland was dearer and more worth to him than the Spanish tyranny—which rules over property, blood, and souls. His manly virtue has left behind his own image, indeed himself, as an inheritance. Him also the whole world must praise and love, who feared the Almighty Spaniard through his ambition, greed, and raging, frantic tyranny. 

And so that I may return to the previous discourse, the widely-famed County of Nassau did not wish to leave you, **Sarcerius** <note>Erasmus Sarcerius (1501–1559), a Lutheran reformer and theologian</note>, in the dark (although you are otherwise sufficiently renowned through your learning). You lived under that Prince who, like the others, was very devoted to God's word, establishing famous schools, out of which especially those might spring and be brought forth who, through preaching, would exalt Christ's Kingdom and root out that of the Antichrist. Just as we still see some today who have come forth from them, who, sheltering under this Doctor Visscher <note>Likely a reference to a specific contemporary theologian or mentor</note>, swim in the wide, wild, desolate sea and among the turbulent people of this world, very skillfully enticing many with the bait of godliness, and catching and bringing them to Christ, the Lord of all Lords. 

When Sarcerius had now won very great honor and high thanks in the Church, and had preserved its state in the County of Nassau in its entirety, he departed to the blessed dwelling place of the elect, and passed from this life at Magdeburg. In the Year of Salvation 1559, and of his age 59.

## The Books published by Sarcerius.

Expositions on Jesus Sirach <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of the Apocrypha.</note>.
Expositions on the Epistles for Sundays and Feast Days, arranged in a sufficiently complete manner and order.
Faithful exposition on the four Evangelists <note>The Gospel writers: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, according to the sequence and meaning of the text.
Exposition on the Acts of the Apostles, quite broad or complete.
Expositions on the Epistles to the Romans, to the Galatians, to the Ephesians, and the Philippians.
Methods, or short ways of teaching, on the principal passages of the Holy Scripture, set down as clear instructional rules for the use of Theologians.
A dialogue explaining the cause of the ancient Synods or assemblies, both general and provincial: Likewise the examinations, and the recently held Synod and examination concerning the Pastors of the County of Nassau, involving the Lord Count William <note>William the Rich, Count of Nassau-Dillenburg (1487–1559), father of William the Silent.</note>, also expressing the acts of the same examination, the report of which can be very beneficial to other lands.
Catechism, or oral instruction, concerning all the disputes and circumstances that may occur in legal proceedings, for the use of Preachers.

On the comparison or the agreement of the true Church and the holy Fathers, but primarily of St. Augustine, on the principal articles of the Christian Religion.
Sermons on the signs and causes which—unless it turns to repentance—foretell many punishments and evils for Germany.
Sermons on Repentance, the manner of restoring discipline, on Councils and Assemblies, on the visitation of Parishes, on the Office of Magistrates and of Subjects, on the Righteousness that avails before God, and good works.
On avoiding the crudities of the old Papists, and the cunning snares and errors of the new Sophists.
Expositions on nearly all the books of the Old and New Testament.
On the Cross or temptation, with the preface of Melanchthon <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a central figure of the German Reformation and collaborator of Luther.</note>.
The Opinion of ancient and modern writers on marriage and the divorce thereof.
The Great Book on the Laws of Marriage.
On the vanity of Scholastic Theology <note>The medieval system of theology and philosophy taught in European universities, which Reformers like Sarcerius often critiqued as overly speculative.</note>, through which the people in times past justified their Religion and teaching; a book arranged in a suitable and common order.
On Brotherly Reconciliation.
A comforting writing concerning his children, to Count Christian.
On the knowledge of the Truth.

# HULDRYCH
## ZWINGLI.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'G' featuring ornate foliate scrolls and architectural elements.</image-desc>
Just as Germany held her Luther, so too has Switzerland held her Zwingli in great esteem. While the sound of Luther’s Evangelical Trumpet spread far and wide across the vast German lands, Zwingli’s voice—hemmed in between the Alpine mountains as if between two walls—could not at first be easily carried to the open plains. But after these cloisters or confining walls were burst through everywhere by this powerful Word, that same sound penetrated the high Alpine peaks (which serve as the walls of Italy) and finally expanded into Italy, France, and the surrounding Kingdoms. At nearly the same time, they both proclaimed the soul-saving doctrine of the Gospel: the latter at Zurich, the former at Wittenberg. 

But Zwingli (as he himself testifies in the book of his articles, through a very learned and excellent Writing) began to preach the Gospel in the Year of Salvation 1516, after he had previously taught that the trade in Indulgences <note>The practice of selling the remission of temporal punishment for sins, a major catalyst for the Reformation.</note> was nothing other than pure deceit and extortion. He drew this from a certain theological disputation which the learned man, Doctor Thomas Wittenbach of Biel—who had been Zwingli’s master—had held some years prior at Basel in the public assembly of the theological High School. 

Zwingli was a man thoroughly and very learned in all kinds of science; through the help and advancement of the three primary languages <note>original: "dryer hooft-talen" referring to Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note>, which he knew well, he searched and handled the Holy Scriptures very usefully. In his own tongue, namely in the Swiss speech, he was considered very choice and eloquent. The City of Rome, being entirely unsettled and as if struck down by these great men and Doctors, placed little hope or trust in her weapons and took refuge in her artful cunning. Through this, she intended to bring them both to ruin and to seize them deceitfully, proposing to give them wealth, honor, and great Titles, so that she might thus draw them into Saint Peter’s fishing-basket <note>A metaphorical reference to the Papacy's attempts to bribe or lure Reformers back into the Roman Catholic fold.</note> for her own service. And just as

<summary>This page introduces Huldrych Zwingli as the Swiss counterpart to Martin Luther, detailing his early education under Thomas Wittenbach and his initial preaching against indulgences in 1516.</summary>
<keywords>Huldrych Zwingli, Martin Luther, Swiss Reformation, Zurich, Thomas Wittenbach, Indulgences, Basel, Rome, Alpine mountains</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the early Reformation; this page continues the narrative regarding the attempts of the Roman Church to influence the reformers and the subsequent theological rift between Luther and Zwingli. The catchword "doot" (death) from the previous page connects to the opening sentence regarding the fate or actions of the reformers.</meta>

-># U L D R I C H<-

just as they <note>The Roman Catholic authorities</note> had wished to make **Luther** their own through cunning practices; so too did the Roman Cardinals (who at that time carried out their embassies to Zurich) approach **Zwingli** quite craftily in the first beginning of the Evangelical Preaching. They sought to win him over and seduce him through money and property (things they otherwise hated to give); but their designs in this were frustrated and came to nothing. For these men, driven by God’s Spirit, despised the world and these Roman *Carnales* <note>original: "Carnales"; meaning fleshly or worldly-minded men</note> or carnal teachings. 

Now, while these two Master Builders were busy faithfully laying the foundations of the Kingdom of the Gospel, a certain discord arose between them. In the continuation of that great work, a heavy conflict emerged over the meaning of the Lord’s words spoken at the Last Supper—specifically the words of Him whom they both proclaimed as the sole source and Master of our Salvation. And although **Luther** was awakened by the Spirit of God, he nevertheless, in the interpreting of those words, insisted upon and maintained I know not what kind of bodily and natural presence and partaking of the Body of Christ, which is entirely contrary to the nature and property of the Sacraments. 

To justify and uphold this opinion of his, he used—sometimes like the Pope, other times using new and strange weapons—arguments by which he defended and stood for the idea that Christ’s body was present everywhere and in all places, just as His Divinity is. This dispute, after the death of both men, was driven with such discord and such violent frenzy by the false **Lutherans**. They not only championed **Luther’s** strange understanding or opinion, but many other more gruesome opinions were devised and invented to protect *Ubiquity* <term>Ubiquity: the theological doctrine that Christ's human body is present everywhere at once</term> or the "being-everywhere" of the body of Christ. Indeed, they did this in such a way that they have nearly uprooted the foundation stones of the Faith, which had been laid by **Luther** with such faithfulness and steadfastness (on account of the hatred they bear toward the **Zwinglians**). 

At this, the Papists <note>A period term for Roman Catholics, often used polemically</note>, being spectators of this strife, laugh heartily. God grant that this dispute of *Ubiquity* or the "being-everywhere" once being taken away, we may all together approach and embrace Him everywhere. That you, **Zwingli**, were the most courageous champion of Religion and the Fatherland, both Religion and the Fatherland express clearly enough regarding you. You did not wish to desert your people (who were wrongly provoked to battle), fearing neither danger nor

<summary>This page describes the failed attempts by Roman Cardinals to bribe Zwingli and details the theological schism between Luther and Zwingli regarding the nature of the Eucharist and the doctrine of Ubiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Huldrych Zwingli, Martin Luther, Eucharist, Ubiquity, Protestant Reformation, Zurich, Lutheranism, Zwinglianism, Sacraments</keywords>

->ZWINGLI<-

death, as befits a faithful shepherd and Teacher, you did not fear. But having awakened and strengthened the spirits of godly Christians—who fight for Religion and freedom—through good hope, exhortations, and consolations, and finally falling dead at the head of the army, you have truly shown yourself to be one who shed his blood for the truth and the common welfare. As often, *Zwingli*, as your adversaries slander this deed of yours, so often do true and godly reasons praise and commend the same as the prompting of your necessity. But the excessive cruelty of your adversaries was noted in this: that they cut the fallen body into four parts and thereafter burned the same to ashes; by which body's living and zealous voice, everyone's hearts had been ignited and stolen (as if by the flame of the Gospel's preaching). The Battle in which *Zwingli* fell occurred in the Year 1531, the 11th of October, he being 44 years old.

### HULDRYCH ZWINGLI.

> It is said Huldrych claimed two things as his own:
> Namely, above all, the Word of God; thereafter, his Fatherland.
> Both of which he was seen to fulfill entirely faithfully;
> Dying for the Fatherland, and burned for God's word. <note>Zwingli died in battle at Kappel; while his body was not burned alive as a martyr, it was quartered and burned after death by his adversaries.</note>

*A good Shepherd and also a strong champion, both*
*Was Zwingli: who united the love of God's word and the land.*
*Whose understanding first prepared these for their defense.*
*The godly mourn the Shepherd; the country mourns the Man.*

# JOHANNES OECO-
# LAMPADIUS. S.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'N' decorated with intricate foliate scrollwork and vine patterns.</image-desc>

Next to his *Zwingli*, with all right, *Oecolampadius* <note>The name is a Greek translation of his German name, Hausschein, meaning "house lamp."</note> takes his own place here, the first true Shepherd and Reformer of the Church of Basel. In learning, in diligence, in stature, position, and age, they were equals to one another; between whom there was such unity of spirit that hardly ever was a holier or more constant friendship, joined through godliness, found between any men. By nation, *Oecolampadius* was a High German, his fatherland being *Weinsberg*, a city in Franconia. At Heidelberg, he became a Master of the Liberal Arts; he studied Imperial Law at Bologna. From *Capnio* or that *Reuchlin* <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a famous humanist and Hebrew scholar often called "Capnio."</note>—whom the Sophists <note>A derogatory term used by Reformers for traditional scholastic theologians.</note> and speakers of "kitchen Latin" <note>A term for the unrefined, non-classical Latin used by medieval monks.</note> could not look upon with good eyes—he learned the Greek language, and from a certain Spaniard, the Hebrew language, at roughly the same time. When he was chosen as a Shepherd or Preacher in his fatherland, he was called to the aforementioned Basel: where the famous *Erasmus* of *Rotterdam* used his advice and joined him to his own work in the printing and publishing of the *Annotations* or notes on the New Testament. He [Erasmus] freely acknowledged (to use his own words):

> *That he was helped in no small way by the labor and assistance of him who was not only gifted with godliness, but also, through great experience in the three principal languages <note>Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note> as well as in Theology, stood out greatly above all other lights.*

He permitted people to call him "Doctor," to which he was sufficiently urged by his friends. The Sophists of Basel, and those there who love dark and obscure teachings, could neither tolerate nor endure him. For he was *Oecolampadius*, that is, the lamp; namely, by bringing forward the Word of God, he uncovered the errors and blindness of the Sophists. Although they opposed him, nevertheless, with the will of the Authorities, he publicly lectured upon and explained *Isaiah*. While he was now exercising the office of Preacher in the Church of God—namely the House of God—he brought the Word of God into it like a lamp for Christians. At which time he [translated] *Chrysostom* on Ge-

# JOHN

<note>The header refers to Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), the primary Reformer of Basel.</note>

...as well as *Theophylact* <note>Theophylact of Ohrid, a Byzantine biblical exegete</note>, like a little bee picking out the flowers of *Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquence</note>, which he published in Latin. Great and unspeakable labor did he bear for the Church of Christ in those times. Against *Johann Eck* <note>original: "Fabrum Eccium"; a prominent Catholic defender against the Reformation</note> he fought alone for the truth; how faithfully and skillfully he carried that out is sufficiently testified by the Acts published by his adversaries, which are more credible than others. So much so that many cities (which at that time desired to see Christ as the light of the world) desired to enjoy and read his services and books as those of a true Reformer and Doctor. Of this, those of *Bern* and those of *Ulm* were the first to testify. He was very skillful and fortunate in settling and removing disputes and discords. In writing, interpreting, teaching, and refuting all kinds of adversaries, as well as in much translating, he gained a great name for himself and much fruit for the Church; his labor on some of the Prophets is highly praised by the learned. In character, he was a very orderly and learned man, also very useful and capable in handling spiritual matters, but he reached only a modest age (though he was worthy of a longer life): and followed his *Zwingli* <note>Huldrych Zwingli, the Swiss Reformer</note> after only a few weeks, for toward the end of November he passed away from this world in *Basel*; he was forty-nine years old. His godly final departure was recorded by *Simon Grynaeus*, a certain and present witness, in a letter written to *Wolfgang Capito*. And truly *Basel* mourned the true Reformer of her religion no less than *Zurich* wept for her *Zwingli*: the great fellowship and joining of whose studies and labors wonderfully advanced the course of the Gospel. So much so that, not unsuitably, many things in the books of *Zwingli*, and again in various books of *Oecolampadius*, are brought together and united. And thus the life and passing of both are properly described for us by *Simon Grynaeus*, *Wolfgang Capito*, and *Oswald Myconius*.

## JOHANNES OECOLAMPADIUS.

Oecolampadius, like a lamp of God, brightly
Shining / has driven the darkness far away.
So that the world has scarcely held such a light within her
As this pious man's virtue / Faith and Hope in life.

The dark night, and the night's ghosts he has driven off,
Oecolampadius, setting forth God's command.
Whose lamp neither the Pope's nor the Sophists' <note>A derogatory term used by Reformers for scholastic theologians who used complex logic to defend traditional doctrines.</note> hate could extinguish,
Since his light is helpfully accompanying every man.

# SIMON GRYNÆUS

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'H' featuring figures within a landscape and classical architectural elements.</image-desc>

The solid and pleasant character which *Simon Grynæus*, a *German* man, displays here, corresponds entirely with his virtues and intellect, which the observer can readily perceive. Among the *Baslers* <note>Citizens of Basel, Switzerland</note> he was a true *Basler*: That is, he was godly, upright, faithful, kind-hearted, and hospitable; he who, above all, greatly loved the simplicity of manners and composure inherent to true *German-Swiss* men. But as he was a *Philosopher*, as well as—I know not whether I should speak according to common opinion—a *Philologist* <note>original: "Philologus"; a lover of words/literature</note>, or a master of the elegance of all arts. Someone might ask why he then occupies a place here among the *Theologians*: But we shall place him in the Holy gallery because he lived holily and in friendship with holy men as a *Theologian*: and observed and described their good customs and teachings. He also publicly taught not only *Philosophy*—though that for the most part—but also *Theology* at *Basel*, and left behind such writings as are worthy of the name of a *Theologian*. He was also joined with *Melanchthon*, *Capito*, *Bucer*, *Calvin*, and many others traveling to the famous Colloquy or conference at *Worms* in the year 1540. And as this man had practiced himself very well in the liberal arts as well as in languages, and through the reading of many authors, both *Greek* and *Latin*, had understood and learned much; it happened that he often gave his judgment thereon, when in those times books by authors of both those languages in all kinds of arts and faculties were being published. Whose advice and judgment even the book-printers themselves seem to have frequently used: And in the planning and printing of their books (which our *Basel*, like a fertile mother, has birthed and brought forth in great multitudes), they greatly desired this man's judgment, as if it were a *Grynæan Apollonian Oracle* <note>A reference to the Oracle of Apollo, implying his word was considered divinely wise and definitive</note>, as is testified by his prefaces and letters placed before nearly all kinds of authors: through which this *Grynæus's* name has been made great and renowned among posterity. Had he lived longer, he would have advanced Literature and *Theology* in no small measure through his fine labor, brave intellect, learning, and judgment. Wherefore he, just like *Zwingli* and *Oecolampadius*, in the flower and the best of his life...

<summary>This page provides a biographical tribute to Simon Grynæus, a German-Swiss scholar at Basel, highlighting his transition from a philosopher and philologist to a respected theologian and advisor to the great Reformers.</summary>
<keywords>Simon Grynæus, Basel, Reformation, Humanism, Colloquy of Worms, Theology, Philology, Book Printing, Melanchthon, Calvin, Zwingli, Oecolampadius</keywords>

# SIMON

life of this world has passed away, being 48 years old, and in the year of our salvation obtained in Christ 1541. In the month of August, at the time when the Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V</note> was firmly equipping his naval fleet or Armada to send to Africa, and the Plague in Germany increased greatly, especially along the region of the Rhine: by which, among others, our *Grynaeus* was snatched away at Basel, and *Capito* <note>Wolfgang Capito, a Reformer</note> at Strasbourg: both excellent in learning; the latter a Theologian by profession, but the former a Philosopher, and both entirely devoted to the Holy Scriptures. Regarding his death, in the preface of the Greek books of Theophrastus, to which is added the praise of his life, the very learned, eloquent man gifted with a diligent judgment, *Joachimus Camerarius*—the light of Germany—has written.

# SEBASTIAN
## MÜNSTER.

SEBASTIAN MÜNSTER is not improperly added here alongside Grynaeus, as he also taught in the Basel Academy or High School. By nationality he was a High German; his fatherland was Ingelheim, and he was a Professor of Theology and the Hebrew language. From a Franciscan <note>A monk of the Order of Friars Minor</note> he became a Christian <note>The author implies a conversion to the Reformed/Protestant faith</note>. He was so learned that he no longer wished to keep house with the "Little Brothers of Ignorance" <note>original: "Broerkens der onwetenheyt" — a derogatory term for his former monastic order</note>; truly a man of vast knowledge, as his books sufficiently demonstrate to everyone. As one who was well-experienced and deeply steeped in all kinds of arts and sciences, as well as Mathematics, his labor in interpreting and explaining the Hebrew and Chaldean <note>term: Chaldean — an older term for the Aramaic language</note> languages was infinite. To print these works during Münster's life, the printing houses of Heinrich Petri and Froben labored to the point of sweating; and he was the first who brought forth for us the Chaldean grammar. Happy indeed are the Basel Doctors and Scribes who, frequenting the print shops daily through their literary works, see the presses with their own eyes and behold their written books printed upon them in great multitudes. By means of this Art of Printing, Basel is today no less renowned throughout all Christendom than Augusta <note>referring to Augusta Raurica, a Roman archaeological site near Basel</note> near Basel was in former times, having sprung from the Romans as a magnificent city (as it appears). And just as Basel delights in *Münster*, so also *Münster* rejoices in Basel, beholding it joyfully; as he has shown and provided us with the depiction of the City of Basel, along with the description of the same and the surrounding lands. There are two singular works among others that have earned *Münster* honor and a name: namely, that Hebrew Bible with a fine, new, and very clear translation, featuring the oldest and best authors in those languages. Likewise, he added annotations drawn from the Rabbinical commentaries or explanations. The other is the *Cosmographia* or Description of the World, dedicated to Emperor Charles V, which was afterwards printed in the languages of many nations, and [won] for himself the

# AMBROSIUS
## BLAURER

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral scrollwork and a profile of a bearded man's head integrated into the design.</image-desc>

THE City of Constance, which took the life of our <note>Jan Hus (c. 1369–1415), a Czech reformer burned at the stake during the Council of Constance.</note> Hus, has in turn brought forth and given to us **Ambrosius Blaurer**, a man descended from an honorable lineage. Drawn by zeal for religion, he previously embraced the monastic life at Alpirsbach; and since he was gifted with a good spirit, having read the books of *Luther* <note>original: "Luteri"</note>, he underwent a change of mind and returned to his own people. That college <note>The monastery of Alpirsbach</note> is situated on the borders of the territory of the Duke of Württemberg. And as the Abbot was fruitlessly urging those of Constance to force him to return to his Order, *Blaurer* broke and frustrated the Abbot’s troublesome persistence and persecution through an excellent piece of writing. Thus, he remained in Constance and thereafter preached the Gospel in his fatherland; the Canons <note>Members of the cathedral chapter</note> gnashed their teeth, for they, being exceedingly wealthy, had already been restrained by the Magistrate. He was present with *Zwingli, Oecolampadius,* and *Bucer* in the famous assembly of *Capito* and at the Disputation held at Bern in the year [15]28. The outcome of which disputation was brought so far and succeeded so well—not only in Bern but also in some surrounding places by order of the Legislators—that the Masses, Altars, Images, and all the idolatrous Roman shops and trinkets were together overthrown. In his fatherland, he served his faithful pastoral office until the time that the Decree or Command of *Charles V* <note>original: "Caroli V."</note> regarding the acceptance of the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim (1548), a temporary religious law intended to reintegrate Protestants into the Catholic Church.</note> or "In-between" was proclaimed—which the godly interpreted as the *Interitum* <note>Latin: "destruction" or "ruin." A common pun among Reformers who saw the Interim as spiritual death.</note> or "Undoing"—and finally took hold even in Constance. This Command threatened all resistors with much suffering. Those of Constance, although for a long time they—foremost among all the High Germans—repelled the *Interim* as a "German Adultery" and appeared more steadfast in this than others, were finally [confronted by] the Emperor’s punitive orders; and a published book, as well as the sharpened swords of the Spaniards (whose cruelty they had previously experienced) directed against them, [causing] such prestige and terror

<summary>This page recounts the early life and reform work of Ambrosius Blaurer, a former monk who became a key reformer in Constance and participated in the Disputation of Bern. It describes the local resistance to the Augsburg Interim and the eventual pressure exerted by Emperor Charles V.</summary>

# AMBROSIVS BLAVRERVS.
<note>original: "Ambrosius Blaurer" (1492–1564), a key figure in the Reformation in southern Germany and Switzerland.</note>

[...it was] their choice that they accepted it <note>referring to the Interim, a temporary religious decree imposed by the Emperor</note>, and thereafter adapted and conformed themselves in matters of Religion. Because Blaurer was in no way willing to allow this, he left his Fatherland: and proved in deed that he possessed a more constant spirit than Constance <note>A wordplay on the city's name, "Constantz," and the virtue of constancy</note> could contain. He allowed his Fatherland to stand before him as the place where the path was shown through which we can reach the true and eternal fatherland. Consequently, his labor and service were very useful to other Churches, in the establishing and well-building of which he persevered with great fruit and praise into his advanced years, remaining a man of God. He departed this world in the 67th year of his age. His learning, piety, godliness, modesty, and steadfastness are highly praised; whom also *Calvin* <note>John Calvin, the influential French theologian</note> held in great esteem for the sake of his excellent learning and experience (so that the testimony of such an excellent and beyond-praise man might be added to this).

*Who was first a Monk, and later taught God's pure word:*

*Following Christ's example as a scent of Salvation for all.*

## JUAN DÍAZ, THE SPANIARD

->While he hastens to renew the doctrines of the faithful Father,<-
->He does not allow the permitted crime to happen beforehand.<-
<note>original: "Dogmata dum properat fidi renovare parentis / Permissum fieri non sinit ante nefas." This refers to Díaz's efforts to spread Reformation theology before his life was cut short.</note>

->For profane Rome was the high priest of crimes.<-

# JOHN DIAZ

Observe this JOHN DIAZ <note>original: "IOANNEM DIAZIVM"</note>, a Spaniard from the heart of *Spain*. After he had diligently embraced the study of literature and *Theology* for many years in *Paris*, he also read the books of *Luther* and many others, as well as the *Holy Scripture*, with all due attention; viewing the *Sorbonne* <note>The theological faculty in Paris, then a stronghold of Catholic orthodoxy.</note> there as filthy and like a *pool* of impurity. In order that he might further advance in the *Holy Scripture* and, with joy alongside others, investigate and present its teachings, he first traveled to *Geneva*. Having stayed there for some days and spoken with *Calvin* (to whom he was very pleasing), he finally came to *Strasbourg*. There, *Martin Bucer* <note>A leading German Reformer based in Strasbourg.</note>, particularly noting the man's learning, care, and diligence, advised the *Magistrate* to appoint this same *Diaz* as a *companion* and assistant in his labors, to help advance the matters that were to be handled in the assembly of *Regensburg* in the year *1546*.

When he arrived in the aforementioned *Regensburg* in the month of *December*, *Pedro Malvenda* <note>original: "Petrus Malvendam"</note>, a *Spaniard* and agent for the *Pope's* affairs in *Germany*, arrived there as well. When he saw *Diaz* in the company of *Bucer* and the other *Evangelicals* <note>A term used for early Protestants.</note>, he stood as if in wonder, seeing how greatly he had changed since he had known him in *Paris*. *Malvenda* meanwhile tormented him greatly therein, knowing that the *Protestants* would boast and exalt more over one *Spaniard* brought to their opinion than over several thousand Germans. Therefore, *Malvenda* spared no effort that might serve to bring his countryman back to the bosom of the mother *Church*: presenting to *Diaz* now hope, then fear, then prosperity, then punishment, persisting equally with prayers and threats. 

But being able to accomplish nothing by those means, *Malvenda* took flight toward evil arts and practices, preparing himself for them; for he imagined that a *Spanish Evangelical* was something rare and strange. *Alfonso*, *Diaz's* brother, an Advocate of the *Roman Rota* <note>The highest appellate tribunal of the Catholic Church.</note>, traveled from *Rome* to *Regensburg* as if enraged and with great speed: with the intent that he would either bring *John* his brother back to the *Roman Church*, or certainly him—

<summary>This page recounts the conversion of the Spanish scholar Juan Díaz to Protestantism and the subsequent efforts by the papal agent Malvenda and Díaz's own brother, Alfonso, to force him back to Catholicism.</summary>
<keywords>Juan Díaz, Martin Bucer, John Calvin, Pedro Malvenda, Regensburg, Spanish Reformation, Sorbonne, religious conversion</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the tragic betrayal of Juan Díaz by his brother Alfonso, following the theological tensions previously established.</meta>

# JOHN

likely to bring about his death; and in order to accomplish this, he devised every kind of cunning in his heart, just as he had been instructed in Italian tricks and flatteries, so that he might more easily deceive his simple and Christian brother. What more shall I say? Among all villains the most villainous brother, while his upright brother was reading his brother's letter with great zeal, had him murdered in a godless, cruel, and unheard-of manner on the twenty-seventh of March, through the service of a piece of a rogue armed with an axe. This horrible deed took place at Neuburg, situated on the Danube twelve miles above Regensburg. <note>Juan Díaz was a Spanish Protestant convert murdered in 1546 by his own brother, Alfonso, a fanatical Catholic.</note> And although *Alfonso* and his godless servant—hellish monsters—were caught in flight and placed in prison, and although the *Palatine of the Rhine, Otto Henry*, to whom *Neuburg* belonged, and others concerned with this act desired to punish these villains, nevertheless no judgment or justice was carried out against these evildoers (since they had great and powerful advocates and patrons). Having been at last released, or more likely set free, they were defended and praised by the Spaniards; indeed, they were exalted to the very heavens for having undertaken such a manly deed in murdering the heretic *Díaz*. From this we can well understand that the Papists have their Cains—yes, many worse than Cain ever was—and alongside them also the Jesuits, who like great trumpeters of praise, defend and highly preach this and similar horrors as a heroic or Roman deed. You, *Malvenda*, <note>Pedro Malvenda, a Catholic theologian who had previously debated Juan Díaz at the Regensburg Colloquy.</note> and you, *Alfonso*, have not so much killed the Christian *Díaz* through your wicked arts and godless weapons, as you have endowed him with a longer life: he who is praised here in great honor and lives eternally with God. As often as this history (which *Sleidanus* <note>Johannes Sleidanus, the famous chronicler of the Reformation.</note> also relates very clearly among other things) is read, so often are the memories of *Alfonso*—sprung from the blood of Cain—and of Spanish cruelties, which are infinite, renewed and brought to mind by those devoted to godliness and the freedom of the Fatherland.

<summary>This page details the 1546 murder of the Spanish Protestant Juan Díaz by his brother Alfonso in Neuburg, highlighting the lack of justice served and the subsequent glorification of the act by Catholic contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>Juan Díaz, Alfonso Díaz, Pedro Malvenda, Neuburg, Regensburg, Johannes Sleidanus, Otto Henry Elector Palatine, martyrdom, fratricide, Reformation history</keywords>

# JUAN DIAZ

Tell me, you Papists <note>A historical, often derogatory term for followers of the Roman Catholic Church</note>, where a brother is freely permitted to murder his own brother: what spirit drives you there? Oh, what a gruesome deed! I issue a warning to all places: guard yourselves against shameful Rome, which loves the craftsman of wickedness.

*Observe this good man, this is the virtuous Diaz,*
*Whom one saw hating the Pope's entirely evil and very godless company.*
*He whom boastful Spain first gave to us: thereafter, a mad*
*and godless Spain took him back from us, to their own small gain.*
