# MARTIN

## BUCER.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'H' featuring floral patterns and scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

Although MARTIN LUTHER’S name is widely praised, you, MARTIN BUCER, are to be considered no less than Luther on account of your piety, learning, labor, care, alertness, and writings. Bucer is a singular instrument of God, as was Luther: both Germans; the latter from Eisleben, the former from Sélestat: both formerly Monks; the latter an Augustinian, the former a Dominican. <note>Bucer was a member of the Dominican Order before converting to the Protestant cause.</note> Awakened by Luther’s voice—speaking boldly before the Emperor at Worms and preaching the Gospel—he was transformed from a Dominican into a Christian. How much labor he devoted to the expansion of Christ’s Kingdom is sufficiently witnessed by his surviving writings and by those who have read them and exercised themselves therein. What great judgment in writing, boldness in counsel, success in the building up of the Church, skill in settling and laying down disputes, and moderation in Disputation he possessed and utilized, his works and Church history abundantly show us. For although he was the Pastor of the Strasbourg Church and had taught Theology continuously for twenty years in that same City, nevertheless, the Churches of other Cities enjoyed this man’s presence, works, and diligence in public colloquies, assemblies, and gatherings. Through his unceasing labor and industriousness, the Kingdom of Christ rose and increased more and more daily. Oh, if only the dispute over the Lord’s Supper <note>The "Marburg Colloquy" and subsequent debates regarding the nature of the Eucharist.</note>—so pleasing to the Papists—between Zwingli and Luther, for the reconciliation of which he performed so much labor, could have been removed by him! And also that those of Cologne, after the frequent and earnest persistence of the Christian Archbishop Hermann von Wied, had accepted this man and had rooted out the capital city of Roman Idolatry among the Germans through this man’s godly services! Which might well have made progress, had the Westphalian Gropper <note>Johannes Gropper (1503–1559), a Catholic theologian and opponent of Bucer in Cologne.</note> remained sitting in his pig-sties, and had he not appeared in the court of the godly Prince.

<summary>A biographical tribute to the Reformer Martin Bucer, highlighting his transition from a Dominican monk to a leader of the Reformation in Strasbourg and his efforts to mediate theological disputes.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Bucer, Martin Luther, Reformation, Strasbourg, Cologne, Hermann von Wied, Johannes Gropper, Huldrych Zwingli, Eucharist, religious disputes</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the reformer Martin Bucer, specifically focusing on his transition from the failed reformation in Cologne to his influential period in England under King Edward VI.</meta>

# MARTIN

who betrayed, imprisoned, and enticed the Archbishop and *Bucer*, with whom he had previously been in agreement, through the unusual delicacies of the Roman whore <note>A common Reformation-era polemic referring to the Roman Catholic Church</note>, which he swallowed so greedily that he could not digest them in his *Westphalian* stomach. That which this Archbishop of Cologne <note>Hermann von Wied (1477–1552), who attempted to reform Cologne but was deposed</note> most earnestly desired but could not accomplish, was carried out by the other Archbishop of Canterbury, *Thomas Cranmer*, Primate in England, a man most distinguished in godliness and learning. He applied all diligence to draw *Bucer* above all, and also *Paul Fagius* of Strasbourg, into England, to which end he wrote them many letters, all urging their arrival there. At that time *Edward* VI reigned, who from his youth was raised in many languages and learning, and well-instructed in godliness through the care provided by the great *Cranmer*. Truly (as the historian himself mentions) *Europe has not had a King of such great hope and expectation in many centuries.* That *Bucer*, who had now rejected the old Roman Colony (or Cologne), was received and embraced with great desire into the new Colony of Christ (or the expansion of His kingdom), namely England, in the spacious gallery of the Canterbury High School <note>referring to the University of Cambridge, where Bucer taught</note>, in the year [15]49. Where, having now taught with great attendance and great fruit for barely two years, he departed this world on the next-to-last of February in the year [15]51, after he had lived 61 years. And he was buried there most honorably and honored with the *Epitaphs* or funeral praises of many learned people. Nevertheless, five years later (after *Edward* had died and the *Marian* storm <note>The reign of Queen Mary I and her restoration of Catholicism</note> was then raging), his body was exhumed and burned to ashes in Canterbury <note>Bucer was actually buried and later exhumed/burned in Cambridge; the author likely conflates the location with Cranmer's see</note>. But four years after that, with *Elizabeth*, the *Most Fortunate among Princesses*, now reigning, the godless and *Marian Edicts* proclaimed against *Bucer* and all other godly men were nullified. The death of this most faithful man damaged the Christian Church not a little, as *Calvin* himself testifies; and how much he attributes to *Bucer* is not unknown to those who have read *Calvin's* writings. Among all, he writes in a letter to *Viret*: *The Church has suffered such immense damage through Bucer's death that as often as I think of it, I feel my heart tear apart.* From this, observer and reader, you may note what kind of man *Bucer* was.

## MARTIN BUCER.

**Your birth, Bucer, Germany boasts of greatly,**
**Happy to have you as a native son.**
**Who, and how great you were, your writings boast even more,**
**Showing the way as one tracks the earth.**

*Behold here Bucer’s face, honorable and diligent.*
*Much care and great labor make the man distinguished.*
*Through learning, habit, and order you are ever bold;*
*Great was this man in the eyes of the Great Calvin.*

# PAULUS FAGIUS. 57

-><image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' depicting a fountain or architectural structure within a garden or courtyard setting.</image-desc><-

**P**AULUS FAGIUS can in no way be separated from *Bucer*, for it was he who, by virtue of his office, was *Bucer’s* colleague, his traveling companion on the road, and his supporter in times of danger. Both of them, departing from the same city at the same time, traveled together to England, the most flourishing and fortunate island in the world. 

But this *Fagius*, although he was born in a humble hamlet—namely Rheinzabern, which is a small town in the Palatinate—and his means were very meager, acquired great knowledge through infinite labor and diligence. He overcame all his misfortunes and earned a great reputation through his wonderful experience and knowledge of the Hebrew language. He had learned this in his early years with great industry, partly from *Capnio* <note>Johannes Reuchlin, often referred to by the Greek form of his name, Capnio.</note> and later partly from *Capito*, a professor of that same Hebrew language in Strasbourg. In these languages, he progressed so much that it is believed no one more learned than FAGIUS had been found in many years prior. 

Because he was an eloquent and very learned man, he was called from Strasbourg to Heidelberg by the Elector Palatine (when he undertook to establish the Church), so that he might show others a certain way and means of spreading the teaching of the Gospel, which was happily begun there. But later (due to the Emperor’s Fortune, then triumphing sufficiently in the war of the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim (1548), a temporary decree ordered by Emperor Charles V to integrate Protestants back into the Catholic Church.</note> or "in-between state"), it completely collapsed. 

At that time, the Church of Germany was in a very sorrowful state, while in England, the Church presented itself with a joyful face. While the Emperor was forcing that harmful *Interim* upon Germany, Roman Idolatry was meanwhile being driven out of England. Thus, these Doctors (pleasing the Emperor the least) were chosen by *Edward*, that most excellent King, and were very acceptable to nearly the entire Nobility, as well as the Common people. But those of Canterbury were only able to hear this learned theologian *Fagius* with wonder for a few months. For after traveling from Strasbourg, where he was a Pastor, to England with his companion, he passed away in November of that same year from a four-day fever, and

# PAULUS

taken up to Heaven: which predecessor was followed the next year by *Bucer*. <note>Martin Bucer (1491–1551), a leading German Reformer based in Strasbourg and later Cambridge.</note> There are many who, not without suspicion of poisoning, believe they both died in that manner. This is not so strange to those who, by some poisonous and pestilential teaching, poison the soul, and easily poison and kill the bodies of those whom they know will discover and abhor the poisonings and sorceries of the followers of Simon Magus. <note>A biblical figure associated with sorcery and heresy; the author implies the Reformers' enemies used both spiritual and physical "poison."</note> Thus, *Bucer's* fellow brother, companion, and assistant *Fagius* <note>Paul Fagius (1504–1549), a Hebrew scholar and Reformer who worked closely with Bucer.</note> felt the same *Marian* cruelty <note>Refers to the persecutions under Queen Mary I of England ("Bloody Mary").</note> against the Godly, not only in life but also after his death, as *Bucer* had experienced, and thus had everything in common with his *Bucer*. Had he lived longer (as he died in the 45th year of his age), the School as well as the Church would have further enjoyed his excellent services, to which he was very beneficial, especially through his singular knowledge of the Hebrew language.

From the Hebrew language, these books were translated and printed by *Fagius*:

*Thisbetes Helia*, which book is called *Thisbi* by the author, named for the multitude of 712 words that are explained in this work. <note>A dictionary of post-biblical Hebrew by Elijah Levita.</note>

<header>THOMAS</header>

# THOMAS CRAN-
# MER.

<image-desc>A large decorative square drop cap "O" containing ornate floral and vine-like motifs.</image-desc>

Among the English, the great THOMAS CRANMER, Archbishop of Canterbury, was distinguished in prestige, learning, godliness, and all manner of virtues. Since he was the Primate—or the first—of England, he was also the first in building up the Church of God, as befits an Archbishop, and was certainly not the least among them. With what diligence and industry, and also with what faithfulness he promoted and stood for the Evangelical cause <note>The "Evangelical cause" refers to the Protestant Reformation movements based on the Gospel</note>, is sufficiently evident and expressed throughout his entire life. Here you see a true Bishop or overseer of the Church of Christ, and an outstanding Shepherd, who rose up against the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era epithet for the Pope</note> as if against a wolf and tyrant, having provided himself with all those weapons by which he intended to restrain and silence the same's cruelty, tyranny, and horrible howling. Under *Edward* VI, a very virtuous King, this most godly Archbishop *Cranmer* possessed great influence, as one who greatly advanced the building up of the Kingdom of Christ through the instruction and counseling of King *Edward*. And as fortunate as England was through King *Edward*, so it was also through Archbishop *Cranmer*; for through the one as the Shepherd of the People, and through the other as the Shepherd of the Lord's Flock, it flourished in both Learning and Godliness. Through the care and persistence of *Cranmer*, *Bucer, Fagius, Lasco, Martyr* <note>Refers to Martin Bucer, Paul Fagius, Johannes a Lasco, and Peter Martyr Vermigli, prominent continental Reformers invited to England</note> and many others were called to England from other lands, so that England—which seemed divided from the Roman world—might also be separated from the Papal Decrees and dominion, and would be subject to Christ as the head of His Church, but to the King as the defender of the Faith, lawfully—that is, according to the laws of God and the Realm. Now that this exceedingly good King has passed away, and that not without suspicion of poisoning, oh, what changes have befallen the affairs of England! For that happy and peaceful State was immediately followed by the raging *Marian* tempest <note>The reign of Queen Mary I, known for the restoration of Catholicism and the persecution of Protestants</note>, which so troubled the island surrounded by the Ocean Sea through the raging blasts of the Roman *Aeolus* <note>In mythology, Aeolus is the keeper of the winds; here it refers to the Pope inciting the "storm" of persecution</note>, mixing together Heaven and earth,

<sig>P iii</sig>
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury, praising his role in establishing the Protestant faith in England under King Edward VI and his efforts in bringing international scholars to the country before the onset of Queen Mary's reign.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury, Edward VI, English Reformation, Martin Bucer, Peter Martyr Vermigli, Mary I, Church of England</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury, focusing on his imprisonment and martyrdom under Queen Mary I, and providing testimonies of his character from fellow reformers.</meta>

# T H O M A S
<note>Thomas Cranmer (1489–1556), a leader of the English Reformation and Archbishop of Canterbury.</note>

that nothing remained free nor unviolated, except only that which the **Antichrist** <note>A common Reformation-era epithet for the Pope.</note>, shut up in his seven-hilled Roman high castle, desired to be free—the King of that Storm. This same power also snatched **Cranmer**, a man entirely upright, kind-hearted, and peace-loving, and threw him into a terrible dungeon. And so in darkness, filth, and want, **Cranmer**, that English light, was deprived of the light for two and a half years. Finally, in London, he was taken up by the cruel flames of the fire, in the year 1556, on the 21st of March. An orthodox and very godly man, and a steadfast martyr, he was burned in the 68th year of his birth. And although his flesh was roasted by the flames, indeed quite consumed, and his bones brought to ashes, yet his heart (according to the writings of **Foxe** <note>John Foxe, author of 'Actes and Monuments' or 'Foxe's Book of Martyrs'.</note>) remained unviolated by the fire. What sort of man and how great a man **Cranmer** was is expressed to us by two excellent witnesses, namely **Andreas Osiander** and **Peter Martyr**. Which **Osiander** wrote thus to **Cranmer**, and that during the time of **Henry VIII**, in the preface to the *Harmony* or comparison of the Evangelists: *I love you both for what you have in common with others—the nobility of your lineage, the dignity of your appearance, the unbelievable sweetness of your manners, your uncommon learning, kindness, and generosity, which you show to all, but especially toward those who love learning—and even more for the inner heroic virtues of your spirit, such as are seldom found in this age. These have been found by me through clear reasoning, namely: wisdom, prudence, courage, temperance, justice, singular affection for the fatherland, exceeding loyalty to the most illustrious King, contempt for earthly things, burning affection for heavenly things, the truth of the Gospel, upright religion, as well as burning zeal for Christ's honor, and finally, an invincible spirit that bears all burdens.* But **Peter Martyr**, in the dedication or dedicatory letter of [his book on] the *Lord's Supper*, writes of him thus: *Whom else could I have chosen for the dedication of this my book, as a champion of the Evangelical truth and of this Sacrament of the Holy Lord's Supper, who would surpass you in godliness, learning, and steadfastness? Truly, no one. There is truly no one among the ancient Fathers whom you have not most diligently studied. There are no books, neither of the ancients nor of the moderns, in which I have not seen with my own eyes, noted by your own hands, everything that serves this entire disputation. Councils, Canons or Rules, the Pope's Decrees or institutions that pertain to this, you have brought together in the most principal parts through such great labor (as far as this matter is concerned); so that I, this...*

# CRANMER.

If I had heard it from others, I would not have easily believed it, had I not, as an eyewitness, found it to be so immediately. Consider now, pious observer and reader, what an excellent Shepherd those Roman Catholics—that is, the greedy, evil wolves—have snatched away from us.

Among his manifold burdens and labors, he composed and published these writings:
The Catechism of Christian Doctrine.
Ordinances of the Reformed Churches.
On the Institution of Ministers.
On the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper, treated with Luther. <note>Refers to Cranmer's evolving views on the Eucharist and his dialogue with Continental reformers.</note>
Defense of the Catholic or Universal Doctrine.
To the Proclaimers of Truth.
The Ecclesiastical Laws in the time of Edward. <note>King Edward VI, under whom Cranmer implemented major reforms.</note>
Against Gardiner’s Sermon. <note>Stephen Gardiner, Bishop of Winchester and Cranmer's primary theological rival.</note>
Five books on the Lord's Supper.
Ordinances or Common Collections from the Doctors in twelve books.
Christian Sermons.

<column-break/>

On the slanders of Richard Smith.
Refutations of Unwritten Verities. <note>A critique of traditions not found in Scripture.</note>
Two books on not marrying a brother’s wife. <note>Relates to the "Great Matter" of Henry VIII’s divorce from Catherine of Aragon.</note>
Against the Primacy or Supremacy of the Pope, two books.
Against the Pope’s Purgatory, two books.
Two books on Justification.
Godly Prayers.
Letters to Learned Men.
He wrote from prison:
Against the Sacrifice of the Mass.
Against the Adoration of the Bread. <note>Refers to the elevation and worship of the Host in the Catholic Mass.</note>
To Queen Mary and several others.
He corrected the English translation of the Bible in many places and provided it with prefaces.

# JOHANNES LASKI.

Here he presents himself to you, beholder: JOHANNES LASKI <note>original: "Ioannes Lascus"</note>, a Pole and a Lord of the Banner <term>Baender-Heere: a high-ranking nobleman entitled to lead men under his own banner</term>, brother of *Hieronymus Laski* (the Envoy of King *Ferdinand* to the *Turk* <note>Referring to the Ottoman Empire</note>), a man of understanding (as *Sleidanus* <note>Johannes Sleidanus, the historian of the Reformation</note> writes) most excellent. Observe this man of ours, eminent in body, in spirit, in learning, in godliness, and in many other Christian virtues: who, in order that he might proclaim Christ, left his entire fatherland; and this godly man preferred to spend his life in exile to preach God's grace, rather than to remain among his own people in favor and high esteem. Such love did he bear toward Christ and His flock, that he tracked down the scattered and wandering sheep—those who were either disturbed from their folds or snatched by the teeth of the devouring Roman wolf—and drew them to himself with a lovely voice, and fed them through the word of God, as becomes a faithful shepherd. Happy are those sheep who encountered their shepherd *Laski* in that time. For when they were wandering in foreign lands, having just escaped the tearing fangs of the hungry and gluttonous wolf, they would very easily have fallen among the thistles and thorns, or some other kind of horrible thieves and murderous snakes, had they not been brought over to the open, lovely pasture of God's word through the labor and care of the vigilant Shepherd *Laski*, and brought into order by a shepherd’s staff—that is, by the laws of Church discipline. Germany is a witness, as is England, which have sufficiently demonstrated the virtues of this shepherd to us. He was called to England by the eminent *Cranmer* <note>Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury</note>, Primate or first of the Kingdom, a learned, godly, and steadfast man: who so advocated for the Church of foreigners <note>The "Stranger Churches" in London for Protestant refugees</note> in that Kingdom, that he bore the abundant fruits of his labor through his godly teaching and good instruction. When the *Marian Tempest* <note>The persecution of Protestants under Queen Mary I of England, 1553–1558</note> would tolerate neither the native nor the foreign Christians, an exceedingly great multitude followed him by water and by land through many dangers, not knowing in what region they should settle. But when *Laski* chose Denmark, he [offered] their small kindness...

<summary>This page provides a biographical sketch of the Polish Reformer Johannes Laski (Jan Łaski), highlighting his noble origins, his leadership of refugee "Stranger Churches" in England under Thomas Cranmer, and his flight from the Marian Persecutions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the reformer Johannes a Lasco, specifically focusing on his theological contributions and his role in establishing the Dutch refugee church.</meta>

# JOHN

experienced [this] very shortly thereafter, as did some of the Lower Saxon cities, and that because of the differing teachings on the Lord's Supper; for he published a very clear treatise on the Lord's Supper (just as *Cranmer, the Archbishop* <note>Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury (1489–1556)</note> did at the same time), both of which they dedicated to King *Edward* <note>King Edward VI of England</note>. These two men were not the least among those who presented this doctrine of the Supper uprightly, clearly, and simply—indeed, in my opinion, better and more aptly than those who, by conceding too much for the sake of maintaining the Church's peace, have gained some praise in this matter. This so-driven *Lasco* was finally accepted by Emden, a city of no small renown in East Friesland: where he settled himself and taught the faithful Christians, especially the Netherlanders, with great loyalty and steadfastness. And in those turbulent times, Emden was like a refuge for the Netherland Christians, the first cradle or nursery of the Netherland Church: from which afterwards, as if from one great congregation, many others sprouted; and the Christian religion, like a fruitful Evangelical seed, spread and grew very strongly and abundantly throughout the Netherlands. Which city and its province so embraced and promoted the Christian religion that *Calvin*, moved by this zeal and love, dedicated his Catechism—such an excellent work—to them. Meanwhile, *Lasco*, requested and recalled by his own people, returned to his fatherland from which he had previously been cast out: in which, after he had taught for a short time, he—whose life was truly nothing but an exile—departed for Heaven, the true fatherland and the resting place of all the blessed. In the year 1560.

-> Though your country enlarges the World, Poland rises even higher above it, <-

# JOHN KNOX.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' depicting ornate scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Scotland's light is here, JOHN KNOX, as one who, in the midst of the Papal—that is, in more than Egyptian—darkness, carried the Torch of God's word before his people and enlightened Scotland. In former times, he had a master in the midst of that darkness (not unknown), namely *John Major* <note>original: "Ioannem Majorem"</note> the Scotsman, very renowned for his subtlety and sharpness of mind: of whom many writings (presented for the display and boasting of his intellect) still remain. In this *Anthropotheology* <note>A term used here to describe a human-centered or scholastic approach to divinity</note> or "mingled godliness," *Knox* being very well practiced, taught the same with great prestige; and in many things he was considered more fortunate than his Master (if such sharpness of mind brings fortune). But earnestly reading through the writings of *Jerome* and *Augustine*, he found in them another Theology: whereupon he regarded that former sharpness of mind as mere dullness and madness, and embraced the simplicity which Christ so earnestly commanded us, as did Paul and the other Apostles. Finally, he felt that the excessive Scottish sharpness of mind was an enemy to both the world and the Gospel, and a Sophistical chatter unworthy of a Christian man. And so, driving from him all darkness, he acknowledged and worshipped Christ—that light shining in the darkness—and issued a very clear confession of Faith: which the mitered, shaven, and light-shunning Bats <note>A derogatory reference to the Catholic clergy, implying they hide from the "light" of the Reformation</note> sought to snap away with their foul jaws and wished to see extinguished. *Knox*, now beloved by many, was captured and destined for certain death. But, God willing it so, he was delivered by a singular means and crossed over into England, where he defended and championed the *Truth* everywhere with great service and fruit, through which *Knox* became very famous. But as *Edward VI* then ruled the English, who held this *Knox* in honor and accompanied him with his favor, a Bishopric was offered to him; *Knox*, being disturbed by this, not only refused and rejected that honor, but also with an earnest and stern oration spoke against that name or title, as having something in common with the *Antichrist*, which he would not allow in the Christian Church: [praising] godliness, modesty, and

# JOHN

and humility, while being very loving. And when the *Marian* Tempest <note>The "Marian Tempest" refers to the persecutions of Protestants in England under Queen Mary I (r. 1553–1558), often called "Bloody Mary."</note> arose at that time, he chose a more secure and stable place in Frankfurt, but finally found his harbor in Geneva. There, teaching and comforting his own, he rightfully performed his office and enjoyed the familiar company of *Calvin* <note>original: "Calvini"</note>. But when *Knox* <note>original: "Cnoxus"</note> returned to Scotland in the year 1559, it is unbelievable with what great wonder this man, awakened by the Holy Spirit, enlightened Scotland through the clarity of the Gospel. And although the French turmoils disturbed the Kingdom, and Satan wrought gruesome things in the innermost parts of the Church; nevertheless, having overcome, he remained standing through his courage in that clear, appearing light. Therefore, he is rightly called here the light of Scotland, who proclaimed Christ, the light of the world, in such thick darkness. In order that this light might always be preserved and held in honor by the Scots, he considered that godliness, modesty, humility, and other Christian virtues must be loved and actively practiced: which also requires Church Discipline <term>Church Discipline: the system of oversight and moral correction used by the Reformed church to maintain order and holiness</term> as the best proponent of that same light. Which the Scots even today (thanks to *Knox's* care) maintain entirely, wholly, and perfectly. Which *Knox*, leaving Scotland, departed toward that light of Christ proclaimed by him, on the 24th of December in the year 1572. <note>The text says 26th of December, though historical records usually cite November 24th.</note>

He left behind these writings:
*To those of London and others.*
*To the proclaimers of the Gospel.*
*How one ought to pray.*
*On the Psalm, dedicated to his mother.*
*Against the Papist Mass.*
*The doctrinal or instruction of the Mass.*
*Of the faith of the Lord's Supper.*
*To the distressed Churches.*

<column-break/>

*To Mary, Queen of Scots.*
*Counsel in these present Distresses.*
*The first blast of the trumpet.* <note>original: "Des hoornes eerste gheblaes," referring to his famous polemic "The First Blast of the Trumpet Against the Monstruous Regiment of Women."</note>
*Appeal or revocation of the sentence of the clergy.*
*To his countrymen.*
*Sermon on Genesis and some others.*

### JOHN KNOX, SCOT.

The Scottish congregation, Knox, first heard your teaching,
And through your advancement was brought into its true being.
To that end, above all, your godliness and heavenly doctrine drew them,
And your praised love for the true <term>Religion</term> <note>The term "Religion" here specifically refers to the Reformed or Protestant faith as opposed to Roman Catholicism.</note>.

*And the bats flee from the clear dawn of day;*
*So also were the dark Sophists <note>original: "Sophisten"; a derogatory term used by Reformers to describe scholastic theologians who used complex logic to defend traditional Church doctrines.</note> driven away*
*By Knox out of Scotland, truly acting as the light of his fatherland.*

# ANDREAS HYPE-
# RIUS.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'O' inside a square frame, featuring intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

Among all the Netherlanders, **ANDREAS HYPERIUS** stands here as a select theologian, whom Ypres—a renowned and powerful city in Flanders—brought forth to us. His father was a jurist. In our *Andreas*, much is remarkably evident to behold: namely courage, virtue, intellect, studiousness, erudition, and experience in many matters, all of which present us with his singular praise. When he was a youth, he grasped—not without the wonder of many—through his outstanding and bold intellect and infinite labor, the most excellent languages and literature, as well as the <term>Encyclopedia</term> <note>original: "Encyclopedie"; here meaning the full circle of universal knowledge or the "perfection" of all arts</note> of almost all arts. This was achieved through the constant maintenance and company of Joachim Ringelberg of Antwerp, a young man wonderfully skilled in all kinds of arts and, if I may say so, experienced to the point of a miracle, who praised *Hyperius* in an excellent, still-extant oration to the Council of Paris. After he had studied in Paris and Leuven, he traveled through Italy, France, Germany, Spain, and England. Finally, having been appointed as a Professor in the Academy or High School of Marburg, he settled there; the school enjoyed his presence first in Philosophy and thereafter in Theology as a select Doctor. How deeply experienced he was in all Philosophy is expressed by his own writings: above all, the very brief, clear, and learned exposition of the *Ethics* <note>original: "Ethica"</note>, or the manner of living virtuously, by Aristotle. This work is very pleasing and useful to read for those studying Theology as well as Philosophy, which he read aloud to his disciples in the same Academy. With what diligence, faithfulness, steadfastness, and how fruitfully he publicly taught upright Theology is clearly demonstrated to us by his labor and life, as well as by the eulogies attributed to him by renowned men; he was one well-instructed in the arts which are commonly called the handmaidens of Theology. This man's writings, on account of their appropriate division and singular organization, teach clearly, and through their eloquence and elegance of words, greatly move and enlighten the reader; so much so that he also the

# ANDREAS

considered very beneficial for Scholastics and Preachers. What *Melanchthon* was in *Saxony*, *Hyperius* was in *Hesse*: he who was very experienced in both political and spiritual histories, the Church Fathers, and also in the writings of the Scholastics and Sophists <note>Sophists here refers to academic philosophers of the Middle Ages</note>. Yet he was mostly an exceedingly diligent reader of the Father *Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom, an early Christian Church Father known for his eloquence</note>, with whose sayings, parables, and divisions he frequently adorned and confirmed his fine books. Because of this, his writings are highly esteemed by those who wish to teach many people; also this man's modesty, patience, and steadfastness are viewed by many with great praise. Which his books also testify, in which a spirit appears free from those affections or inclinations that commonly lead men astray, as he also writes of himself in the Treatise on the Investigation of Holy Scripture, never to cease: *This is my art, this is my armor of war, these my war-councils, through which I protect myself against my encroaching enemies, withstand them, and commonly overcome them: I* **KEEP SILENT**, *I* **ENDURE**, *I* **HOPE**. This is the praise of *Hyperius*, who is praised as a never-to-be-fully-prized Theologian by a certain Theologian of no small name. This man, worthy of praise in all times, died in the Year of Christ 1564, the first of February, at the age of 54, after he had taught at *Marburg* for 22 years. Of which this little verse, being short and elegant, expresses the year of his passing:

->*Flandria quem genuit Hassia nostra tegit.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Flanders bore him, our Hesse covers him."</note>
->That is:<-
->*Whom Flanders brought forth, the Hessian earth covers.*<-

He is buried in the same place where his very great friend lay while he lived, *Gerardus Noviomagus* <note>Gerard Geldenhouwer, a Dutch humanist and historian</note>, according to the same gravestone at *Marburg*:

->*Here rest the bones of Gerard of Nijmegen:*<-
->*As also those of Andreas Hyperius opposite them.*<-

### ANDREAS GERARDUS HYPERIUS.

**Not Ypres, Hyperi, formerly your fatherland,**
**Gave you the name that you received much earlier from God:**
**Much less did light fate cast such a name to you by hand,**
**But Virtue, *Andrea*, names you according to her full decree.** <note>The poet plays on the Greek word 'Andreia' (manly virtue/courage) and the subject's name, Andreas.</note>

*Manly virtue, Andrea, gave the name strongly,*
*Which womanish Rome considers weak and adulterous.*
*The city of Ypres, fertile in men, gave this one from her bounds*
*As a mighty man: because of which he now lives peacefully.*

<header>WOLFGANG</header>

## MUSCULUS.

WOLFGANG MUSCULUS <note>1497–1563, a prominent Reformed theologian</note> ought not to be esteemed among the least of men. Born to humble parents in Dieuze in Lorraine, a city bordering Alsace, he became highly distinguished through labor, diligence, and understanding of the Holy Scriptures, as well as through science and knowledge of divine matters; he was chosen as a faithful servant of Almighty God’s word. From his fifteenth to his thirtieth year, he lived in the Palatinate <note>A historical territory of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, and during the rise and rebirth of Evangelical truth, he lived as if under its very wings, protected by the diligence and support of the Bailiff of Lützelstein, Reinhard of Rothenburg. During those times, the "Harpies" or fair-faced birds of prey <note>A metaphorical reference to the Roman Catholic authorities</note> of the Bishopric of Metz would have gladly devoured him because of his study of divine truth, which was then first rising out of the abyss and to which he was inclined; however, they feared to strike their claws into the lordship and territory of the Palatinate. For this protection, he also greatly thanked the Count Palatine, Frederick (called "the Pious"), in his dedication or introductory letter which he placed at the beginning of his *Common Places* <note>original: "Locos communes"</note> or *Order of Godly Doctrine*, a large, very learned, and well-wrought book written in his old age. He was called by the people of Strasbourg to be a minister of the Divine Word and was a very beloved fellow-brother to Bucer <note>Martin Bucer, a leading Reformer in Strasbourg</note>. Afterward, he came to Augsburg to preach the Gospel and was joined with Urbanus Rhegius, a most learned theologian, in the year 1531. This year was the first after the assembly so famous for the *Augsburg Confession*, which many today fight for while others fight against it. In that office, he was very useful to the Church of Augsburg and to the matters of God, as well as to students of Church history, translating the ancient Teachers and Fathers from Greek into Latin <note>Musculus was famous for his translations of Eusebius, Cyril, and others</note>, and also bringing to light commentaries for the further clarification of the light of the Gospel. But Musculus, by the Emperor’s edicts and commands, was forced to leave Augsburg due to the most sorrowful war of the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim of 1548, an imperial decree attempting to integrate Protestants back into Catholic practice</note> or "In-between state," and came to Bern, where he was with great longing de—

# WOLFGANGUS

honored, he was received very kindly; for fourteen years he taught the Holy Scripture there through his writings, always striving to bring forth something by which God's Kingdom and His name might be enlarged and glorified, until he exchanged his life for death—namely, in the 66th year of his age and the year of Christ's birth 1563.

Truly, this Musculus contributed much to the Christian Churches: through whose labor many books and commentaries were brought to light, and in that time passed through many hands, not without great prestige and admiration. Therefore, this Musculus is not laughed at by the Papists <note>A common 16th-century Protestant term for Roman Catholics</note>, especially since he—through his *Common Places* <note>original: *Locos communes*; a foundational theological textbook</note>—opened and set before the eyes of the whole world that ugly peddler’s shop: namely, the taxation of the Apostolic Chancery, which is a gathering place for all villainous acts and horrible blasphemies. Because of this, those fat Roman elephants with insatiable bellies seem to rave and rage, just as if mice (tormenting them to the point of madness) had crawled into their long-snouted noses. Great, then, is this Musculus to be esteemed, who penetrated into the very treasure chamber and the innermost depths of the Roman Pluto <note>A reference to the Roman god of the underworld, here used to satirize the Pope</note> and King of Purgatory.

->The writings of Musculus are these:<-

Commentaries on Genesis.
Commentaries on the holy Psalter, 2 Volumes.
Commentaries on Matthew, collected in 3 Volumes.
Commentaries on John.
Commentaries on the letters of Paul to the Romans and Corinthians.
Commentaries on the letters to the Colossians, the 2nd to the Thessalonians, and 1st Timothy.
*Common Places* <note>original: *Loci communes*</note>.
Commentary on the Ten Commandments.
Some sermons against the Papists' Mass.
*Anti-Cochlaeus I* <note>Johannes Cochlaeus was a major Catholic opponent of the Reformation</note>, or against Cochlaeus, against a certain booklet defending the Priesthood

<column-break/>

as the sacrifice of the new law.
Whether it be permitted for a Christian man, knowing the Evangelical doctrine, to make himself a partaker of Papist superstitions and false worship through outward attendance: 4 *Dialogues* or conversations.
3 Dialogues on the Council of Trent.
How far a Christian man ought to endure injustice.
Against the impure Catechism.
*Dialogue* on the swearing of oaths, against the error of the Anabaptists <note>original: *Weder-dooperen*</note>.
On the German War <note>The Schmalkaldic War</note> arisen in the year 1546: 6 *Dialogues*.

## W O L F G A N G U S  M U S C U L U S .

**Such** a being was formerly Wolfgang Musculus there /
From whom Great and impure Rome was terrified.
For their brood's clutching-claws / he did not value a hair:
Therefore they were his, even though they be small. <note>A play on his name 'Musculus', which means 'little mouse' in Latin.</note>

He who bites through the Great Hunter's snares, deceitfully spread,
Is considered here a very small Mouse:
But where he lays his nest even in the Pope's treasury:
Is he not a great Mouse who brought such darkness to light?

# JAMES
## FABER.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' featuring a seated figure, likely a scholar, in a classical architectural or garden setting.</image-desc>

This is the <term>Faber Stapulensis</term> <note>Latin: Jacobus Faber Stapulensis; known in French as Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples (c. 1455–1536)</note>, who, although he sprang from a humble homeland and was but a small man due to his short and slight body, was nonetheless gifted with a great spirit and a magnificent intellect. He was a <term>Philosopher</term>, knowing not the darkness that his era brought with it, but rather the clarity, teaching in such a way that gave great light, understanding, and knowledge in the interpretation of the learned writings and proverbs of the Philosophers. There was no part of <term>Philosophy</term> which he did not understand very well: and he taught others readily and aptly, despising those unsuitable, clumsy words, maxims, and <term>sophistries</term> <note>original: "VVif-redenen," referring to empty or circular arguments</note> which are understood by none but the barbaric dullards and those who love obscure minds. In <term>Metaphysics</term>, or supernatural things, he instructed and planted in the refined mind that which harmonized and agreed with Christian Philosophy. He was followed by a great multitude of select young men who pursued Philosophy with great diligence as the pathfinder to fruitfully attaining greater understanding and scholarship. This "Husbandman of spirits and minds," this little man, could not be tolerated or endured in the Royal City of Paris by the <term>Sorbonnist</term> <note>Refers to the faculty of the Sorbonne, the conservative theological center of the University of Paris</note> Giants; there, with great agitation of their spirits and the flailing of their arms, forging upon the anvil of human wisdom, they greatly feared fine, godly teachings over their traditions and hypocritical shops of human institutions. And this <term>Faber</term> held a great reputation and renown for the sake of his virtue and scholarship, not only among the French, but also among the Italians and Spaniards. For this Faber, whom the Sorbonnist <term>vain-theologians</term> <note>original: "Matæologi," a Greek-derived pun used by humanists to mock "theologians" as "talkers of vanity"</note> or "wise-in-their-own-conceit" Doctors also persecuted, even the King of France, <term>Francis</term> the First, wrote letters to the Parliament of Paris—and that even at the time when he was imprisoned in Spain, the capital of ambition—through the recommendation of his sister <term>Margaret</term>, Queen of Navarre, a most gracious Princess, who, for the sake of his virtue, very much [loved]

<sig>S ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: loved (bemin-)</meta>

<summary>A biographical tribute to the French humanist Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, praising his intellectual clarity and his defense by King Francis I and Margaret of Navarre against the conservative theologians of the Sorbonne.</summary>
<keywords>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, Faber Stapulensis, Humanism, Sorbonne, Francis I, Margaret of Navarre, Philosophy, Metaphysics, Paris</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples (Latinized as Jacobus Faber Stapulensis), focusing on his exile and his influence on the early Reformers.</meta>

-> J A C O B V S <-

beloved. Of whom more is read in the works of the excellent historian Sleidanus <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), a chronicler of the Reformation</note>, who also rightly added his judgment regarding this:
> *It is entirely, he says, as if innate to the nature of the theologians of former times, that they take to tormenting learned men. The cause of which is that they notice that their own ignorance is being despised.*

But this wise and artistic *Faber* (or Smith) <note>Faber is Latin for Smith</note>, disregarding the Scholastic and characterless, artificial Theology, departed from the City: having fallen into danger of his life, the aforementioned Queen Margaret <note>Marguerite de Navarre, sister of King Francis I and a patron of humanists</note> rescued him, and placed *Faber* in the city of Nérac (situated under her jurisdiction in Aquitaine), where he lived for many years. From the school of this pious man, many learned men who loved virtue and godliness have sprouted; among others, *Farel* and *Calvin* testify to this. When the latter [Calvin] greeted *Faber* out of courtesy in Nérac in the year 1534, he (being still young) was received by the good old man with great joy of spirit, [Faber] foreseeing that *Calvin* would yet be an outstanding instrument who would rebuild God’s Kingdom in France. And just as those who were somewhat more advanced were well-instructed in *Faber’s* school, so were the children and youths very well raised in the school of *Maturinus Corderius* <note>Mathurin Cordier, a famous educator and Calvin's teacher</note>, who was a very diligent instructor and had sat in the "school-sand" <note>A metaphor for the teaching profession</note> for fifty years. In this *Cordier’s* school, *Calvin*, as it appears, was considered outstanding. Let be those who accuse *Faber*—a Master of the most upright Philosophy and an embracer of the truth—of Heresy: among whom that word-smith *Jovius* <note>Paolo Giovio, an Italian historian</note> writes thus:
> *When Faber was now old, and had taken up the Holy Scriptures, he was not far from the Lutheran poisonous Heresy: so that he, being old in that reflection, died unhappily.*

But you, godly observer, shall understand this judgment of *Jovius* over *Faber* as being full of praise <note>The author implies that being called a "heretic" by Jovius is actually a badge of honor</note>. Now let others boast of their Socrates, Themistius, Alexander, or Simplicius: the French have a *Faber* who teaches good morals and expresses them with a righteous life, whom they praise as an enlightened Philosopher and a godly interpreter of the Holy Scripture. To whose honor this pleasant Christian verse still remains, which shows us that which he desired to be done after his death.

# JOHN
## CALVIN.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' containing a woodcut illustration of a scholar or theologian seated at a desk with a book in a vaulted room.</image-desc>

This is that great CALVIN, a Frenchman, his fatherland being **Noyon** in **Picardy**: for whose praise and honor no one has ever lacked material, provided they wish to be held as righteous and godly judges of things; they must confess that *Calvin* was one who, according to his worth, has never been sufficiently praised by anyone. And is he not to be praised above all praise, from whom Virtue herself—which is the very source of true praise—was able to learn virtue? So that he (whoever he may be) wrote well, that *Calvin* was the one,

> From whom Virtue herself could learn Virtue. <note>Original Latin: "Ipsa a QUO potuit VIRTUTEM discere Virtus."</note>

-> That is: <-

> Him, from whom Virtue could learn Virtue.

If anyone wishes to diligently examine *Calvin's* mind, learning, and life, they shall truly find in this single man everything that is most to be desired in a godly teacher: the gifts of his nature were select, his learning immense, his labor incredible, his care, vigils, and virtue surpassing. All of these have made *Calvin* renowned and famous throughout the entire world. This is the man whom the most learned and pious *Philip Melanchthon* <note>A key German Reformer and collaborator of Martin Luther.</note> named "The Theologian" by excellence or distinction, as they say. And he did this while *Calvin* was still a young man, treating divine matters most excellently, as witnessed by that outstanding work, the *Institutes of the Christian Religion* <note>Calvin's seminal work of systematic theology, first published in 1536.</note> or summary, dedicated to the King of France, *Francis I*: through which *Calvin* showed himself in his youth to be a man and a select theologian. Although he had previously studied Law at **Orleans** under *Pierre de l'Estoile* <note>Latin: Petro Stella. A famous French jurist and teacher of Calvin.</note>, the French legal scholar who was at that time essentially the foremost in France in that study, and in which he progressed so well that he was regarded more as a Doctor (serving in a Doctor's office) than a disciple; nevertheless, the study of Law—promising wealth, honor, and everything that men greedily hunt after—could not [keep him] from Theology (far-

<sig>T</sig>
<meta>catchword: ach- (likely "achtende" - regarding/considering)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces John Calvin, detailing his origins in Picardy and his early transition from a brilliant law student in Orleans to a world-renowned theologian through his "Institutes." It emphasizes his immense intellectual gifts and the high regard in which he was held by contemporaries like Melanchthon.</summary>

<keywords>John Calvin, Noyon, Picardy, Philip Melanchthon, Orleans, Pierre de l'Estoile, Francis I, Institutes of the Christian Religion, Reformation theology, legal studies</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues a biographical account of John Calvin, focusing on his education and his character as a reformer. It follows a discussion of his early life and transition toward the Reformation.</meta>

# JOHN

(the inner essence of external things) could not turn away. At Bourges, under *Melchior Wolmar* <note>original: "Melior Volmaro"</note>—a German lover of the true Religion and very renowned for his learning—he learned the Greek language, just as he afterwards learned Hebrew at Basel, maintaining great fellowship with *Simon Grynaeus* and *Wolfgang Capito*. But who is it that surpasses *Calvin* in sharper and more certain judgment? Who is more learned in Divine matters? More experienced in the handling of Church affairs? Who is more ready and pure in writing and teaching? More excellent in care, labor, and watchfulness? Who has contended more successfully with the adversaries of the Gospel? Given more certain counsel in doubtful matters? Who is more courageous in dangers? More steadfast in adversities? More diligent in prosperity? Who has so despised honor and wealth? Who has loved frugality, moderation, and above all, a life of godliness as much as *Calvin*? Thus, in *Calvin* were all the virtues required in a Theologian: so that here, those who *proclaim the Gospel of Christ and edify the Church by feeding the Lord's flock with words and deeds* have an example of a most faithful shepherd and most godly teacher. But to some, *Calvin* seemed to be stern. From this they must learn that levity was far removed from *Calvin's* theology, and it is found that he was a stern punisher of vices. And some (through evil rumor) call him cruel. How is it even believable, I ask you, that he was cruel, he who so diligently read *Seneca* on Clemency <note>Refers to Calvin's first published work, a commentary on Seneca's "De Clementia" (1532)</note>, and adorned the same with learned and manly commentaries while still quite young? He who so often despised the cruelty of the Papists and offered them his own gentleness? But this is beyond our current purpose; and if one were now to speak of the fruit and the labor of *Calvin*, truly it shall be greater than was ever brought forth by the new or old Theologians. So useful has this man's service been to the Church of Christ, that in almost the entire Christendom no place is found that is not filled with *Calvin's* labor. Of which the primary witnesses are his fatherland *France, also Germany, Italy, Transylvania, Poland, England, Scotland, Spain*, and other Kingdoms, Provinces, and Cities, in which the Church of Christ has been strengthened and gladdened through the writings and counsel of *Calvin*. There have been almost no heresies in any Kingdoms against which he did not, through God's word,

<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of John Calvin, following the discussion of his early influence and theological battles.</meta>

-># CALVIN<-

as if with a two-edged sword, he has fought fortunately. So much so that he may not unjustly be called the Christian Hercules, who everywhere hunted down and destroyed cruel and fierce monsters <note>A metaphor for heresies or theological errors.</note>. Just as the ancient Church had her *Augustine*, so the new Church in this century has her *Calvin*: whom some, not without reason, have even placed above *Augustine* in certain matters. If sharpness of mind is sought (which *Augustine* seems to have pursued too much in many things), then *Calvin* shall be esteemed no less than *Augustine* in that regard. If we look for Judgment—which is the sauce or salt of all writings and words—we shall find that such appeared more in *Calvin* than in any other. If we look for Theology that is firm in all writings and consistent throughout itself; that is remarkably present in *Calvin*: whose books of *Retractions* or of corrections <note>original: "Retractatie"; referring to Augustine's famous late-life revisions of his own work</note> (the virtue of which is nonetheless attributed to *Augustine*) have never been seen among us. There are yet other things to note in *Calvin* which others did not have in common with him: as far as the style and manner of writing, as well as the structure and sequence of fine arguments are concerned, we shall find that he used that nature of writing which best becomes and suits a Theologian. In his fine Orations there is a singular gravity and sobriety: and a very beautiful, well-constructed, manly composition, which through its weight penetrates and moves the minds of those who read with judgment. In which manner of writing he has had hardly any equal. And as this man was a singular Patron and defender of the truth; it is no wonder that someone has placed him before all others in defending the truth. There is one *Gregorius Massovius*, who (as a disturbed Jesuit named *Campian*—that fighter who seems to have Mars sitting on his boastful tongue—earnestly relates) esteems one *Calvin* more than a hundred *Augustines*. And certainly (if one were to speak of the truth) who is there with any good understanding who would not prefer such a champion of the truth over many others (who either fight against it or draw it into doubt)? He has had many and powerful enemies, which is nothing new, for enemies have never been lacking for those who stood for the truth. This great man died of consumption <note>tuberculosis</note>, which he contracted through much staying awake and immoderate fasting, in the year of our Lord 1564, in the 55th year of his age, having been brought into this light <note>born</note> in the year 1509, on the 10th day of July: On which day of the...

It concludes by noting his death in 1564 due to the physical toll of his rigorous academic and ascetic lifestyle.</summary>

Moderation always accompanied Calvin,
Which he expressed through his being and his hands.
From whom virtue itself learns virtue and good customs.
Rome, he was your terror, under whom you bowed.

Each praises *Calvin's* knowledge in *God's* word so clear:
Therefore he is to Rome like a lightning strike.
So wise: that he alone surpasses a host of many men:
The wicked arrows of the Heretics he parries with the flashes of his shield.

# JOHN CALVIN.

## Against the Raging of the Papists and Heretics.

->To<-
->the Righteous Reader.<-

*Calvin is said to be a great heretic,*
*Godless, unlearned, a weak adulterer, and lazy too,*
*A sorcerer, wicked, cruel, greedy, and full of injustice,*
*But who counts all these faults? He who is full of malice.*

How can he be Evil, who hates what is wicked?
How can the father of virtue be such a wrongdoer?

<note>The author uses a rhetorical device common in the 16th and 17th centuries, listing the standard "black legends" or slanders used by opponents of the Reformation to discredit Calvin's character.</note>

# GUILLAUME
# FAREL.

<image-desc>An ornate historiated woodcut initial 'G' depicting a figure, likely a scholar or cleric, seated at a desk with a book.</image-desc>GUILLAUME *FAREL* from Dauphiné <note>A province in southeastern France</note> is here rightly joined with his friend *Calvin*, as one who came forth from the school of *Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples* <note>original: "Iacobi Fabri"; a pioneering French humanist and theologian</note> and followed Christ the supreme master as a servant of the Divine Word before all others. Through the labor and diligence of this man, as well as that of *Pierre Viret*, the torch of the *Gospel* <note>original: "Euangelij"</note> was first lit in the City of Geneva (*which hoped for light after the darkness* <note>original: "Post tenebras lux," the motto of Geneva</note>). The night of errors was driven away, and all the shadows of the darkness of *Papist* <note>A term used by Reformers to refer to the Roman Catholic Church</note> superstitions and all kinds of idolatry were scattered. What a wakeful and faithful shepherd of the Church of Christ he was can be clearly proven to us by a single example: namely, that through his counsel, earnest exhortations, strong persistence, and declarations, *Calvin*—who had hardly thought of such a thing before—was kept in Geneva, where Farel desired and had him as his companion. In order that this may appear more clearly, it should be expressed through the choice and very eloquent words of the *Theologian* <note>Theodore Beza</note> who described *Calvin's Life* for us, reading as follows: *Therefore, when Calvin had visited them there (namely Farel and Viret) in passing, as is customary among friends, Farel—being a man endowed with a heroic spirit—urged him with many words in vain to labor with him in Geneva rather than travel further. And when Calvin would not willingly grant him this, Farel said: "I declare to you, who now withdraw to your studies, in the name of Almighty God, that if you do not uphold and take up this work of God with us, it shall happen that the Lord shall curse you (as one who seeks not so much Christ as yourself)." Calvin, being terrified by this fearsome pronouncement, gave himself over to the will of the Church Council and the Magistrate. Following their voices with the consent and choice of the congregation, he was not only chosen as a Preacher (which he had first refused) but was also appointed a Doctor and teacher of Holy Scripture, which was the only title he allowed.* From this we can see how much *Geneva* is beholden not only to *Calvin*, but also to *Farel*. Through whose prayers, strong persistence, and threats it came to pass that those of *Geneva* have...

# GUILLAUME FAREL

had and called him their *Calvin*. *Farel* was a vehement preacher: whose zeal and spirit in preaching was so great, and his voice and words of such great power, that he seemed not so much to speak as to thunder, by which he so moved and stirred the minds and spirits of the listeners that he drew out now these, then those inclinations and open demonstrations from his audience, according to the nature of the matter he was handling or proposing. Now, as he possessed great influence through his experience in Church matters and preaching, the Churches that had recently cast off the yoke of the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Papacy</note> were greatly advanced and assisted by his labor. Likewise, in the year 1542, he proclaimed the Gospel of Christ in the city of Metz. And he was finally a Minister of the Church of Neuchâtel: who just before (*Calvin* departed from the world) went from Neuchâtel to Geneva in the 75th year of his age to greet him who was now very ill, with whom he had always lived in great unity. And the following year *Farel* followed his *Calvin*, that good and godly old man, who, after he had at all times rightly and well served the office of a true Preacher, received the reward for all his labors.

Farel has published:

# PIERRE VIRET.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'D' containing a figure in a classical or scholar's robe, possibly sitting at a desk, surrounded by ornate foliage and architectural elements.</image-desc>

This is P I E R R E  V I R E T <note>original: "Petrus Viret", a Swiss Reformer (1511–1571) known for his gentle persuasion</note>, who follows his *Calvin* and *Farel*. Born in *Orbe*: which small town, being under dual lordship, acknowledges the legal jurisdiction of both *Bern* and *Fribourg* <note>Orbe was a "shared lordship" (bailliage commun) between the Protestant Bern and Catholic Fribourg</note>; a man who was well-experienced in the Holy Scriptures and most excellent in eloquence. The French Churches were established and confirmed in all the surrounding lands through this man’s care, vigilance, and labor, as well as that of *Farel*. Because he was so articulate and eloquent in expressing God's words to the people, the Church of *Christ* enjoyed the fruits of his labor abundantly. In speaking he was so lovely that he always made his listeners diligent and eager to hear him; such great power and harmony was in his words, so attracting the ears and minds of the listeners, that he even held those little inclined toward Religion, and otherwise impatient of discipline and morals, compliant and submissive through his lovely speech and enticement: so much so that they, as if fixed to his mouth, seemed to desire a longer sermon. These three chosen ones <note>Calvin, Farel, and Viret, often called the "Triumvirate of Geneva"</note> were at one time shepherds of the Christian Church: each adorned with a singular gift, through which each of them was most excellent in the edification and building up of God's Church. So that just as one commonly called *Calvin* a learned, *Farel* a zealous, so also *Viret* a very eloquent Preacher. Through these three most faithful servants of *Jesus Christ*, persisting so entirely diligently, the Christian Religion was extended far and wide; with writing, Preaching, comforting, and good counsel, they were very beneficial to the Church. When the Harvest of the Gospel in *France* was very great and abundant, *Viret* was also called to this as a laborer: whose labor and fruits *France* has greatly enjoyed, and having enjoyed them, carried them away and even now preserves them well. Neither ruses nor snares, peril nor danger, nor even the cruel French wars, could frighten this man away from his zealous purpose: but through the midst of all enemies he very diligently preached and increased *Christ's* Kingdom. So that the great Church of *Lyon*, and other surrounding provinces, through the glorious work which he [performed] in spreading God's...

<summary>This page provides a biographical tribute to the Reformer Pierre Viret, highlighting his exceptional eloquence and his collaborative work with Calvin and Farel in establishing the Protestant faith in France and Switzerland.</summary>
<keywords>Pierre Viret, John Calvin, William Farel, Orbe, Lyon, Reformation, eloquence, Protestantism, France, Switzerland</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Pierre Viret, a key figure in the Reformation, focusing on his literary style and his death in 1571.</meta>

# PETER.

brought the word to pass, loved this man dearly, and read his writings with great pleasure, mocking the Papacy of that time as they were very fit and suited for that purpose. For *Viret* was one to whom the mystical and wondrous Papal theology <note>original: "Papistische Theologie"</note> was very well known. In explaining and clarifying this through many books, he often moved the reader to laughter because of the strange and ridiculous miracles that it brings forth. And when he had further separated the Heathen theology from the books of secular writers, he so skillfully compared and reconciled it with the pseudo-holy Papal theology, as if he had placed these Roman religious services as two parallel lines running together, and as if they were entirely at one with the horrible idolatry of the ancient Romans. So much so, that we consider there to have been hardly anyone among the old theologians who so uncovered and brought into the open that mystical kingdom of the Roman Jupiter <note>A satirical reference to the Pope as a pagan deity</note>, knotted through with obscurities, as this *Viret*. This can be shown above all from that one *Cento* <note>original: "Centone"; a literary work made up of quotations from other authors</note> or gathered patchwork (to pass over many others) of the Farce of the Mass <note>original: "Missen Battementspel"; likely referring to his satirical work on the Mass</note>, pulled together from the ancient poets: wherein he pierces and delights the reader—especially those experienced in poetry—with a new kind of pleasure, just as that widely renowned Roman Beehive <note>A reference to "The Beehive of the Romish Church" by Philips of Marnix, Lord of Saint-Aldegonde</note> does for the Netherlanders. Now when this *Viret* had well served the office of a preacher and herald of the Divine word in Geneva, in Lausanne, and in the surrounding places, mostly in France, and had awakened many people through the power of his spirit, and also moved many to the knowledge of God and conversion through the fluency of his words and the sweetness of his voice: he finally passed away in the Kingdom of Navarre, entering that Kingdom which has no end, in the year of Christ, King of all Kings, 1571, and of his age, 60.

---

->The writings left to us by *Viret* are these:<-

Expositions on the works of the Apostles.
On the origin of the new and

<column-break/>

old idolatry, five books, in which is shown wherein the old superstitions or false beliefs are distinguished from the new, and wherein they agree.

-># VIRETUS<-
<note>Latin: Viret. Referring to Pierre Viret (1511–1571), a prominent Swiss Reformed theologian and close associate of John Calvin.</note>

Which images or relics are true and which are false.

Who the true mediator is. <note>A central Reformation theme: Christ as the sole mediator between God and humanity, rather than saints or Mary.</note>

On the origin, progression, use, dignity, and power of the ministry of the Divine Word and the Sacraments; and of the divisions that have arisen because of them throughout all of Christendom, especially in these our times, and the means to reconcile the same. In 18 Books.

On the true Word of God, the Sacraments, and the Ecclesiastical ministry. In 21 books.

1st Book on the corrupted Sacraments.

On the corrupted Sacrament of Baptism, and on the use of holy oil and consecration. In 1 book.

On the Mummery-play <note>original: "Battament-spel," a derogatory term for a theatrical or nonsensical performance</note> of the Mass; a *Cento* <note>A poem made up of various verses from other authors</note> or gathered together from the ancient poets.

Treatise on the use of the Angelic Salutation <note>The "Hail Mary"</note>, and on the use and abuse of chapels.

Comforting letters.

An exhortation made to the Believers living among the Papists, especially the Courtiers and those placed in positions of honor, [showing] how they provoke God to anger, and [how] they should not boldly desire to put their lives in trial and danger.

V. Popish Physics, or dialogues, whose names or titles are: I. Medicine or Mercury. II. Bathhouses or Charon. III. Blessed Water. IV. Holy Fire. V. Alchemy or Pluto. <note>Viret often used satire and "physics" (natural philosophy) as a metaphor to critique Roman Catholic rituals.</note>

Christian instruction and the principal teachings of the Holy Scriptures, in which the main points of the true Religion are recounted familiarly through dialogues.

Dialogue of the Confused World.

The acts of the true and false followers of Christ in the Pope's Church.

The similarity and dissimilarity between the Holy Supper and the Mass.

On the communion of the Believers.

A familiar explanation of the Our Father.

On the nature and various kinds of vows and of the Divine Laws.

Christian Disputations on the state of the Deceased.

# AUGUSTIN
## MARLORAT.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'A' depicting a seated figure, possibly a scholar or biblical figure, within an architectural frame.</image-desc>AUGUSTIN MARLORAT, a native of Lorraine <note>original: "Lothringer"</note>, became a Christian out of an Augustinian monk through the singular grace of God. Through his alertness, diligence, and learning, he earned the reputation of a most excellent shepherd of the Church of Christ. In his conduct, quietness, and godliness, he was a most choice man—even though these virtues are considered vices by the monks. Therefore, *Marlorat* also believed that one should by no means live among them. After he had laid the foundations of Religion in France, he finally came to Lausanne: which is a city under the sovereignty of Bern, situated on Lake Geneva, and very famous for its Theological School and printing house. *Marlorat* thought this place most suitable for his studies and exercises, as one who dearly loved the labor and service of godliness. Having been chosen as a servant of God, he preached and presented the upright doctrine of the Gospel (which spits out all mixing of human wisdom and the pool of stinking superstitions) here by this well-known Lake Geneva—where the flow of the *Rhone* <note>original: "Rhodani"</note> pours its waters through unmixed. There, he maintained and pastured the sheep of Christ with that blessed nourishment of souls. How much he advanced and grew in God’s word in a short time, through the unbelievable love he bore for the Holy Scriptures, is shown to us by his own studies and labors. Who must not wonder, seeing what he brought to pass in this regard: a work truly exceedingly heavy, published in his Commentaries or general expositions on Genesis, on the Psalms, on Isaiah, and the entire New Testament. These were produced with wonderful art, order, brevity, and clarity, containing within them the sentiments of the most upright Doctors or Christian teachers of the Church. So much so, that this General Ecclesiastical Exposition may not unjustly be called a library or a fullness of books. For the Doctors, whose sentiments and...

<summary>This page introduces Augustin Marlorat, a former Augustinian monk turned Reformer, highlighting his scholarly work in Lausanne and his influential biblical commentaries.</summary>
<keywords>Augustin Marlorat, Lorraine, Lausanne, Lake Geneva, Rhone, Reformation, biblical commentaries, Augustinian Order</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the reformer Marloratus, following the mention of his scholarly contributions and the spread of the Reformation through France.</meta>

-> AUGUSTINE <-

<columns>
judgments recounted in that work could serve the joyful and diligent reader as a sufficient library or a complete collection of books, to which doctors' opinions he has also occasionally added his own views. This work, being printed with the very beautiful letters of *Stephanus* <note>Robert Estienne (Stephanus), the famous Parisian printer known for his elegant typography.</note>, moves the reader all the more to wonder. Now, as the Evangelical teaching flowed with the current of the *Rhone*, that exceedingly swift river, through the Lake of *Geneva*, it moved through France toward the west; and as it swept over the neighboring places and landscapes, making them fertile with its living waters, there subsequently came forth a joyful and abundant harvest everywhere. Laborers were requested and called upon (among whom *Marloratus* was the most prominent) to prune and gather this Evangelical harvest, and to orderly arrange and distribute it for the time to come. He was also present at the widely renowned colloquy of *Poissy*, held in the year [15]61, where certain articles concerning the schism in religion were treated; from which not much fruit came forth: except that, since the cause of the Christians became better known and understood, many who previously condemned them through ignorance, then felt and judged more justly regarding the godly. In the following year, when *Rouen* was besieged and taken by the Antichristians <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Roman Catholic forces.</note>, our *Marloratus*, as a shepherd of the *Rouen* Church, was taken prisoner along with three of the most prominent men of that city; whose lives were taken by a noose by those who, allied with the Roman Pope, the tyrant of souls, showed themselves by this deed to be his chosen servants. A true Martyr then, for it is the cause, and not the punishment, that makes one so. He died in the year 1572, on the 30th of October, at the age of 56.

# ROBERT
## ESTIENNE

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap "M" containing a detailed woodcut illustration of two figures (possibly scholars or biblical figures) in a landscape setting with architectural elements.</image-desc>

With good reason does ROBERT ESTIENNE <note>original: "Robertus Stephanus," the famous Parisian printer and scholar (1503–1559)</note> take his place here: a Frenchman among his fellow Frenchmen, a scholar among the scholarly, and a godly man among the godly. He enjoyed their friendship, and through his printing house—which he regarded as a divine gift and a useful invention—he brought to light their pious labors and books for the benefit of all Christendom. He may rightly be included here. *Estienne* was a good, honest, God-fearing, learned, and industrious man. He is the honor of all book-printers; he seems to have surpassed all printers who are now, who have been, and who shall ever be. So much so, that whenever honor and praise were to be won among all printers, he met them as a victor—not only by his name *Stephani*, but also in deed a *Stephanus* <note>A play on the Greek word 'Stephanos' (Στέφανος), meaning 'crown' or 'wreath' given to victors</note>, carrying away the honor through his crown, just as victors and kings are accustomed to do, being chosen and preceding them all. Kingly, then, is this man, and worthy to be the printer to King Francis I <note>original: "Francisco den eersten"</note>. To him the King himself, like all learned men, bore a singular favor and love. And just as today students and lovers of the arts and sciences behold those exceptionally beautiful letters—especially the Hebrew and Greek characters—with wonder, so shall posterity be struck dumb upon seeing them. Scholars shall praise the immense care and diligence he applied to the correct printing of his books, while others shall praise other qualities in him. The virtues of his spirit, such as his greatness of mind, the integrity of his life, and his contempt for outward things, arouse wonder above all in those who possess a manly spirit. But these things might seem to be raised here outside of our purpose or intention, as we are dealing here with the theologians and scholars who have fought against the Roman <term>Antichrist</term> <note>A common Reformation-era term used by Protestants to refer to the Papacy</note>. Now, how greatly this *Estienne*, through his diligent and industrious works, has labored to combat and uproot the kingdom of the *Antichrist*; [he] has...

<summary>This page provides a laudatory biography of the master printer Robert Estienne (Robertus Stephanus), praising his technical skill in typography and his contributions to the Reformation through scholarly printing.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Estienne, Robertus Stephanus, printing history, typography, Francis I of France, Reformation, Greek type, Hebrew type, Antichrist</keywords>

# ROBERTUS

<meta>catchword: take</meta>

nor does that singularly excellent edition of the Bible prove otherwise, explained with short and general—that is, agreeing with the truth—annotations: for which reason he had to endure and withstand a heavy and continuous Sorbonnist <note>The Sorbonne was the theological faculty of the University of Paris, known for its strict orthodoxy and opposition to the Reformation.</note> war. He, in his answer to the Censure or rebuke of the Parisian Theologians, addresses the well-willing readers who seek Jesus Christ our Savior in truth, as follows: *In the first place, I am forced to express the feelings of my mind when I remember what a war I have had against the Sorbonne for about twenty years now; I cannot wonder enough how such a small, weak little man could have been powerful enough for it? But the Lord has snatched me out of the hands of that Parisian Synagogue or assembly, and out of all expected danger from the common people, who were incited by the Sorbonne. For as often as they saw me, now drifting above and then below, they mocked and insulted me with pleasure in back alleys and in their drinking bouts, saying: "It is over with him, he is ensnared and caught in the Net of the Theologians, he cannot escape it: indeed, even if the King wanted to pull him out, he cannot do so."* But at last he left Paris and departed to another place where God led him. Thus at last the minds of the Sorbonnists were ignited with great wrath, and that which they had muttered inwardly, they spat out at Stephanus <note>Robert Estienne</note> with all their might. How learnedly, openly, and Christianly he answered the aforementioned Censure of the Parisian Theologians—who had slanderously accused the Bible printed by him—the gifted reader with any judgment and understanding can easily see and notice: through which he showed himself to be no less a good theologian than a learned man, truly in a different manner than that which Erasmus, who was ready on both sides <note>A reference to Erasmus of Rotterdam's perceived neutrality or "middle way" in the religious conflicts of the time.</note>, wrote in response to the Censure of the Parisian "wise-seeming" Theologians. But you, Stephanus, who surpass all other book-printers in learning, godliness, art, and spirit, have expressed with deeds that which you had indicated with words when you were thus quarreling with the Sorbonnists: *What would they have suffered one to print? Unless I had been willing to undertake the "Summulas" or heap-work of Mandreston, the "Logic" of Enzinas, the "Moralia" of Angestus, the "Physics" of Majoris, the Breviary, and the Missal <note>These refer to traditional scholastic textbooks and liturgical books which the Sorbonne approved, contrasted with Estienne's scholarly Greek and Latin Bibles.</note>. Then my labor for the Holy Scripture and the intelligent would have been in vain, for whose sake I have employed it until now, and the remainder of my life with a firm pur—*
