# STEPHANUS.

taken up, being suited and destined for that purpose. With great praise, *Stephanus*, you have carried out what you undertook and promised. Learned, good, upright, and godly men shall always speak well of you. How faithfully you have loved the Christian Religion is witnessed by the joyful sign displayed by your printing shop; your lovely Olive Tree, as a desired and fruitful tree, has brought forth the sweet fruit of the messengers of Evangelical peace—especially that of *Calvin*—which we have plucked and enjoyed with great delight. <note>The "Olive Tree" was the famous printer's mark of the Estienne family, often accompanied by the motto "Noli altum sapere" (Be not high-minded).</note> Here, beloved observer and reader, you have to see and read what was produced by *Stephanus*: who, after he had lived uprightly for 56 years, departed on the 7th of September in the year 1559 to that Heavenly fatherland (which was dearer to him than his Parisian one). <note>Robert Estienne fled Paris for Geneva in 1550 to escape religious persecution from the Sorbonne theologians.</note>

A New Common Gloss, that is, explanations gathered together from the Ecclesiastical Writers, on the Gospels of *Matthew*, *Mark*, and *Luke*.

# SLEI-
## DANUS.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'O' with floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

Among all those who have stood as legates in the assemblies and before the Princes of the Christian cause, advocating with orations and writings, and who through their learning have shown themselves to be both good theologians and skilled <term>political men</term> <note>The original "Politicque mannen" refers to statesmen or those skilled in civil administration</note>; I believe this place must be held by those in the midst of them (as among the chosen). Of these, the first is Johannes Sleidanus; the second, Johannes Sturmius; the third, Philip Marnix, Lord of Saint Aldegonde. Far-famed has been the name of Johannes Sleidanus: who illuminated the obscurity and humble reputation of his fatherland through the light of his fine learning and the radiance of his piety, as well as through his descriptions of ecclesiastical and public affairs. Sleida is a town located in the land called Eifel, stretching toward the borders of the Duchies of Limburg and Jülich, situated not far from the city of Düren, which is widely known for its capture by the powerful hand of Charles V <note>Holy Roman Emperor Charles V captured Düren in 1543 during the Third Guelders War</note>. From this humble Sleida, the great man Sleidanus emerged. After he had laid the first foundations of letters in the schools of his fatherland, he betook himself to that magnificent School of Paris: in which he grasped and learned those languages and arts which are worthy of and becoming to a free-born man. Diligently applying himself to Jurisprudence, he shortly obtained the title (as they call it) of the honor of a practitioner of the Law. To this he added eloquence, as the companion of Jurisprudence: by the help and judgment of which arts Sleidanus later appeared more courageous and learned in the handling and describing of public affairs. Before he departed from Paris, he brought to light that far-famed chronicler of French affairs, <note>original: "Froissardum"</note> Jean Froissart, through a brief and well-considered summary, and very learnedly translated it into Latin. Finally, he came to Strasbourg, the far-famed city, and that precisely at the time and occasion when the Republic was flourishing with many good theologians and men highly distinguished and experienced in political or civil government.

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Johannes Sleidanus as a premier scholar and diplomat, detailing his birth in the Eifel region, his education in Paris, and his eventual arrival in the intellectual hub of Strasbourg.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Sleidanus, Eifel, Paris, Strasbourg, Jurisprudence, Jean Froissart, Charles V, Reformation diplomacy</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the historian Johannes Sleidanus, focusing on his diplomatic career and the reception of his historical writings during the Reformation.</meta>

-># JOHN<-

to whom **Sleidanus** <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), a famous historian of the Reformation</note> was very dear and pleasing, demonstrating his intellect and judgment alongside those who strived for the good governance of the congregations. Therefore, the city of Strasbourg became like a second fatherland to this man. It was there that he brought to light, in Latin, that excellent historian **Cominaeum** <note>Philippe de Commines, a Burgundian/French chronicler</note>—who is not to be placed behind any of the ancient predecessors—dedicating the work to both the Duke of **Saxony** and the Landgrave of **Hesse**, and highly commending the whole of Germany in that state of affairs. He was sent as an envoy on behalf of the Protestants to the King of England, and thereafter, by the specific mandate and command of the Republic of Strasbourg, to the Council of Trent and to the King of France. Great and excellent indeed was **Sleidanus’s** reflection and diligence in describing his history, especially regarding the events that occurred in respect to Religion. For this purpose, he chose Strasbourg as a suitable place; in which city, as if from a far-seeing height, he was able to look upon and observe everything that occurred and was brought about in various kingdoms and lands (and especially in Germany), incorporating whatever served his purpose into his book to preserve it for posterity. This history has earned **Sleidanus** a great name and praise among all people; today it is read and praised by the Germans, French, Italians, Spaniards, and many other peoples and nations into whose languages the History has been translated. Consequently, the fact that it has been printed in so many different scripts shows us the worthiness and excellence of the same writer. The Latin language is expressed therein very learnedly and finely; therefore, the same History is frequently presented by schoolmasters to the coming youth for reading. When the Papists, or rather the cunning Jesuits (acting like apes who mimic the Gospel), perceived this, they pushed forward and urged their own **Laurentium Surium**. By means of this Carthusian monk, who prattles on in a certain strange new manner, they think they have stopped **Sleidanus’s** mouth. How many ridiculous whims, childish flatteries, and things written without reason or judgment have been brought forth by this man, is known to those who have read him with insight and understanding. I will say nothing of the vanity and shameless lies in which this monk is particularly distinguished. In your history, **Sleidanus**, are many things which the Political and Ecclesiastical ruler, with great earnestness and

# SLEIDANUS

and observe the diligence which—in seeking out the antiquity of the ceremonies or of the Papacy (if I may say so)—must move the Papists to wonder and the Christians to laughter. Especially where you, *Sleidanus*, bring out from the corner and reveal the Roman "bull-stall" <note>A derogatory reference to the sale of Papal Bulls and indulgences</note>, the "Lambs of God" <note>original: "Lammerkens Gods"; likely referring to Agnus Dei wax medallions</note> or the consecrated wax, the blessed rosaries, the Bishops' choir-mantles, the fabrications and consecrations, the deifications or canonizations, and the strange "Mass-making" <note>A pun on the Catholic Mass and "making a mess" or distortion</note>, and similar profound things. These things, which belong to that mystical and hidden theology found in the books of ceremonies, you have described quite skillfully and with great devotion, portraying everyone to the life wherever the matter requires it. You have done this with the intent that everyone may understand what the religion of the Papists is chiefly driven by, and how the Roman Popes—who mix heaven and earth together through their Bulls and wars—ensnare the eyes of Princes and catch the simple or the inexperienced. Let everyone read your History, *Sleidanus*, both the common people and the rulers of the Church, who wish to know and recognize the Pope's artful deceit and the clever counsel, ruses, and snares of the enemies of the Church and the community. And just as you formerly commended your *Comines* <note>Philippe de Comines, a chronicler Sleidanus translated</note> to the Germans, so today we commend *You* and your *History* to the Christians (the faithful description of which formerly did not fail to impress even Emperor Charles V himself). We commend it especially to the Netherlanders—namely those who have to deal with the Spaniards, which Spaniards (under the cover of the Roman religion) are concealing all their mercies <note>i.e., showing no mercy while pretending to be pious</note>. Let *Ludovicus Avila the Spaniard*, *Lambertus Hortensius* (otherwise a learned man), and that *Laurentius Surius* now be silent before you, along with others who cannot endure the clear-shining torch of your History and hide themselves in their lurking-holes. So renowned is your name that your enemies, through all their skill and practices, have not been able to hinder it; even though the conceited theologians *Fontanus* and *Pontanus* oppose and fight you, as does *Bartholomeus Latomus*, who (just as *Alanus Copus* has babbled) has written a History—who would not laugh at this?—of the "eleven thousand lies of *Sleidanus*." Thus you have obtained an eternal name through your History, which the wise man *Johannes Sturmius* confirms for us through this, his verse:

# JOHANNES

> Sleiden was my first, Strasbourg became my second Fatherland:
> Died for the sake of life, through my History made known.
<note>This couplet suggests that while his physical life ended, his "life" or reputation continues through his famous historical writings.</note>

Sleidanus passed away in the year of his age 50, and of Christ’s birth 1556.

## SLEIDANUS HAS
->brought to light:<-
<note>The phrase "brought to light" refers to the publication of his works.</note>

Two Orations: one to the Emperor Charles V, the other to the German Princes as the States of the Empire: In which the Papacy and Religion are treated.

Translated into Latin the small Catechism of Martin Bucer.

The Commentaries of Comines <note>Philippe de Comines (c. 1447–1511), a Flemish-French diplomat and historian</note>, concerning the life and dealings of Louis XI and Duke Charles of Burgundy, translated from French into Latin.

Also the same author’s five books of Commentaries on the Neapolitan War, with a brief explanation of the matters and the Author’s life.

Concerning the Republic or Commonwealth of the French and the office of Kings, two books by Claude de Seyssel <note>original: "Claudii Sesellii"</note>, translated into Latin.

The Summary or compendium of the teachings of Plato regarding Republics and Laws.

A brief historical compendium of Froissart <note>Jean Froissart (c. 1337–c. 1405), chronicler of the Hundred Years' War</note>, the excellent French writer, translated into Latin.

The twenty-six books of Commentaries on the things that occurred in Religion and the Commonwealth

<column-break/>

under the Emperor Charles V. This History has been translated into many languages and printed in various places. Likewise, it has been drawn into a brief compendium. Also arranged in tables, through which—with order and distinction—what was contributed to the civil governments can be seen. This History was finally augmented by the very learned historian and jurist Justinus Gobelerus with new brief arguments for each book, and a continuation from the Year of Christ 1556 to the Year 1558.

Concerning the Four Monarchies or Empires, a very elegant book. <note>This work became a standard textbook for teaching universal history for centuries.</note>

Sleidanus would have published even more historical writings which he then had under hand, had death not prevented him. And it is held by many that for the sake of bringing these Histories to light, this excellent man Sleidanus was poisoned and done away with.

# JOHANNES STURMIUS

It is only right that **Sleidanus** <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), a famous historian of the Reformation</note> should be joined here with his fellow **Sleidanian**. For the small town of *Sleida* <note>Schleiden, in modern-day Germany</note> also brought forth the great **Johannes Sturmius**. *Sleidanus* the historian was born in the year 1506; *Sleidanus* the philosopher and Christian orator <note>referring to Sturmius</note> was born in the following year, 1507, and is rightly placed alongside him. For what reason should *Sleidanus* be separated from *Sturmius*? They who—to use the verse of the very learned and exquisite poet *Georgius Calaminus*—were united by a single fatherland:

> *Is Sleidanus joined by Fatherland, he who was a neighbor in home and birth,*
> *And a companion in art to Sturmius,*
> *A rival in the Muses, and in the love of Cicero?*
<note>original Latin: "Est junctus Patriâ, lare qui vicinus & ortu, / Et socius arte Sturmio, / Æmulus & Musâ fuit, & Ciceronis amore / Sleidanus?"</note>

->That is:<-

> *One in fatherland, inhabitant and neighbor together,*
> *A companion in the Arts with Sturmius,*
> *A zealot for learning, loving Cicero's aims,*
> *Sleidanus, capable and entirely wise.*

To this I add the bond of Godliness, which, as the perfect bond, strengthens the minds of men. Now, when *Sturmius* had heard good and diligent teachers—such as *Arnold of Einatten* (whom he, moved by a singularly grateful spirit, remembered during the founding of his school at Strasbourg)—at Liège, in the College of *Jerome*, commonly called the *Fratres* or Brothers <note>The Brethren of the Common Life, a communal pious group</note>, whom those giant-like *Polyphemus-esque* <note>A reference to the one-eyed giant of Greek myth, used here as a derogatory comparison</note> Jesuit brothers cannot well endure. He also traveled to Paris, as if to a marketplace of all good arts. There he practiced the Greek language and joined philosophy with eloquence; through his diligence in these, *Sturmius* later became very renowned. And he departed for Strasbourg (which is considered quite a "Paris" within Germany). And when he was chosen there as Rector of the Great School, the city became even more famous on his account.

<sig>Z iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: His</meta>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Johannes Sturm, focusing on his educational legacy in Strasbourg and his theological defense of the city's religious traditions against internal opposition.</meta>

# JOHN

He founded and adorned his school with those arts through which one can win honor and a name, so much so that Strasbourg has named this *Sturm* the father of her schools. To this day, there is a very great influx of students, nobles, and the children of lords and princes to this institution. This city is rich in all kinds of sciences and the observations and examples of excellent men: both of which make the young youth more learned and more skillful. This German *Nestor* <note>A reference to the wise, long-lived counselor from Homer's Iliad, signifying Sturm's seniority and wisdom.</note> was highly esteemed for his learning, eloquence, experience in affairs, counsel, and prestige, to which he was also naturally suited; through whose assistance the Church, the Republic, and many princes found themselves well-served. How godly and excellent a theologian he was, and how experienced in ecclesiastical matters, can be clearly testified to us by his orations titled *Antipappi* <note>original: "Antipappi" — Sturm's polemical writings against Johannes Pappus.</note> published against *Pappus the Ubiquitarian* <note>Johannes Pappus (1549–1610), a Lutheran theologian who clashed with Sturm over the doctrine of Ubiquity (the omnipresence of Christ's body).</note>; what a strict advocate for the Strasbourg Church and defender of the Republic's dignity and honor he is found to be is clearly expressed by the following oration (which I have wished to add here to his praise).

> *Thus he speaks in Antipappus II: I carry a concern for our city and our honorable Council—namely our fathers, both those still living and those who have died during these forty years that I have lived here in the city, as well as those who departed this life twenty years before that, who were the origin of the renewal of religion <note>The Reformation.</note>. They were able to endure and bear the Emperor's anger and disfavor, and they sheared away and despised the Pope's dominion and over-great power. For the renowned praise, honor, and glory of all these men (which they won and obtained for all posterity through their foresight, strength, and steadfastness), I am anxious; and I rejoice that through you people, I have been given cause to remind them to defend themselves against all slanders, and not to yield to you, who condemn their confession (for which their ancestors were prepared to die)? And do you hold for certain that our ancestors advocated a false teaching and were the maintainers of false shepherds?*
>
> *As for me, I desire that these my old man's words, and this seventy-year-old voice, be recounted and heard throughout all Christendom, and I shall not suffer them to be suppressed by any kind of danger.*
>
> *I feel the same regarding the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, 1530.</note>, that it must be preserved, which Philippus Melanchthon described by the Prince's command: which all the Princes signed in turn: and by their re-signing strength—*

<summary>This page describes Johannes Sturm's role as the "Father of Strasbourg's Schools" and his fierce defense of the city's Reformation heritage against the theologian Johannes Pappus. It includes a passionate excerpt from Sturm's "Antipappus," where he reaffirms his commitment to the Augsburg Confession at the age of seventy.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Sturm, Strasbourg, education, Reformation, Johannes Pappus, Ubiquitarianism, Augsburg Confession, Philippus Melanchthon, religious conflict</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Johannes Sturmius, focusing on his defense of the Augsburg Confession and his literary legacy.</meta>

# STURMIUS

confirmed, and expressed better than before, also well-knit with good reasons, strongly concluded, and steadfastly defended: of which Confession, Melanchthon understood the true sense and meaning better than those "foolish youths," as Cicero calls them <note>original: "sotte jongelinckens". Sturmius is likely citing Cicero’s disdain for inexperienced or arrogant young rhetoricians.</note>.

Such a one and so great a man was *Sturmius*: whose honorable life in the sight of all men, and in the midst of God’s Church, conducted through so many years, and publicly praised by the testimony of so many excellent men of all states and conditions, adorned by so many—I say—both common and singular eulogies from illustrious Lords and Princes of Germany, France, and England (whose favor to win is no small praise), the slanders of the adversaries and the envious barking of haters were easily suppressed and frustrated by him. He died in Strasbourg, in his second Fatherland, being over eighty years old; a worthy and exquisite man, *Sturmius* was an ornament not only to the city, but to the whole of Germany.

## THE WRITINGS
->of Sturmius are these:<-

Letters on the strife, need, and danger of *Germany*: and upon whom it depends that the means of unity between both parties is not reached.

II. Letters on the reformation of the Churches and Religion, one to the Cardinals and other chosen Prelates, the other to Cardinal Jacobus Sadoletus <note>Jacopo Sadoleto (1477–1547), a Counter-Reformation cardinal known for his correspondence with reformers.</note>.

Some writings on the Lord’s Supper, old and new, by Bucer <note>Martin Bucer, the primary reformer of Strasbourg.</note> and others, printed with his prefaces.

The same author's *Antipappi* III <note>A series of polemical works against Johannes Pappus.</note> against Dr. Johannes Pappus’s love and condem-

<column-break/>

-nation of Christians.

The fourth *Antipappi* of the three previous parts. I. Admonition. II. Counter-preface. III. Against Osiander <note>Lucas Osiander the Elder, a Lutheran theologian.</note>.

*Pappus Elenchomenos* I. &c. <note>Greek/Latin: "Pappus Refuted."</note>

To Lucas Osiander.

Letter of defense against Jacobus Andreae, another "Egyptian Rod" <note>A biblical metaphor for a harsh oppressor or a plague.</note>, Doctor of Theology at Tübingen.

The Confession of Augsburg accepted at Strasbourg.

There are others of that nature against the Ubiquitists <term>Ubiquitists: those who held the Lutheran doctrine that Christ's body is present everywhere (ubiquitous), a major point of contention with the Reformed tradition</term> or "body-everywhere-placers," published by Sturmius.

->Thus far concerning what pertains to Theology.<-

## JOHANNES STURM.

Sturm, a lover of the wisdom of both God and man,
Was praised as the honor and worth of the Strasbourg school.
In this citizen of the Fatherland, his eloquence
Delighted them all together, as a choice and excellent gift.

*A very wise Orator, and a teacher entirely faithful,*
*Is this German Nestor <note>A reference to the wise, long-lived counselor from Homer's Iliad, implying Sturm was a venerable elder of the Reformation</note>, agreeable to Princes.*
*Steadfastly he corrected the Pope and the barking of Pappus <note>Johannes Pappus (1549–1610), a Lutheran theologian in Strasbourg who frequently clashed with Sturm over religious doctrine</note>.*
*The honor and glory of the German People is Sturm altogether.*

# PHILIPS MARNIX, LORD OF ST. ALDEGONDE.

->Look here, pious spectator, there shows himself<-
**PHILIPS MARNIX, Lord of St. ALDEGONDE**,
born in Brussels, whose name is renowned throughout all of Europe. A noble and most excellent man. Highly distinguished in lineage, spirit, learning, eloquence, virtue, ingenuity, piety, and in services and good deeds. This Aldegonde is the one to whom Pallas <note>The Greek goddess Pallas Athena, representing wisdom and strategic warfare.</note> had revealed all her art and science, whose mind piety had built, and whom the Freedom of the Fatherland had entirely possessed and occupied. This man was given to the Netherlands at an opportune time, in which the Spaniard—having seized and elevated in his mind the desire to rule the same Netherlands (as an ancient pretext for waging war)—very heavily oppressed and stifled it. Having brought the Netherlands under subjection, the Spaniard envisioned a paved and prepared path to invade and claim for himself the lands of neighboring Princes. Which ambitious and tyrannical wickedness he covered with the cunning of his Religion. Now, as the oppressed Netherlands was sighing, seeking out men to be its protectors and advocates; Behold, the Lord of *Aldegonde* presented himself among the first: who from Germany, where he had previously fled, moved into Holland (*as the Fortress of Netherlandish Freedom*) in the year 1572. Where, driving affairs quite strongly, he showed himself to be not the least champion of the Fatherland’s Freedom: who above all was dearly beloved by that Illustrious *Prince of Orange, William the Great, Father of the Fatherland*, whose manful virtue shall at all times be remembered and highly praised. It would be unbelievable to say how much labor and danger he bore and endured for the sake of that priceless Freedom (during that five-year Hollandic war). The Netherlands now catching its breath; Brabant, and Flanders, together with the other Provinces, publicly embracing Freedom, *Aldegonde* has obtained a great name among all. He has, by various Kings, Princes, and Republics—

<sig>Aa ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: —publics (ken)</meta>

<summary>A biographical tribute to Philips of Marnix, Lord of Saint-Aldegonde, highlighting his intellectual prowess and his pivotal role as a diplomat and advisor to William of Orange during the early years of the Dutch Revolt.</summary>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Philips van Marnix, Lord of Saint-Aldegonde, focusing on his diplomatic skill and his literary contributions to the Reformation.</meta>

-># PHILIPPS<-

been sent many times on embassies. Everything that could be desired in an ambassador could be beheld in one such as *Aldegonde*. His Oration, read by everyone, which he delivered as Legate at the Diet of *Worms in the year 1583*, expresses at length the customs, powerful cunning, and plots of the most ambitious and cruel Spaniards. Amidst his manifold anxieties and the handling of weighty matters, he was always writing or translating something which he thought useful and profitable for the Church or the community. Through this manner of writing, he uprightly strengthened the minds of those who were godly and loved the Fatherland's freedom. The understanding of historical knowledge that existed in this man appears in that writing wherein he deals with the Spanish "Wit" <note>original: "Wit" - meaning their intent or ultimate aim</note> or purpose, at which they aim all their arrows, and toward which they very skillfully apply all their ingenuity, counsel, deed, and plots: those who propose and promise themselves the *Monarchy*, or the dominion of the entire world, and dare to undertake everything boldly. Wherein, in a prophetic manner through his political prognostications or foresights, he gave out many things beforehand: which England, Poland, France, and several other Kingdoms can testify to have happened and occurred in such a way afterward. He served in the first place of Counsel or Burgomaster <note>The chief magistrate or mayor</note> within Antwerp at the time when *Alexander Farnese, Prince of Parma*, besieged the same city. Having exercised the office of a good Burgomaster there, and upon leaving it, he so pleased himself that he also lived for others: always writing as well as bringing to light something which might be beneficial to the Church and the government of the community in future times. He labored long and much on the expression of the Psalms: which he translated from the Hebrew (as he was experienced in the three principal languages <note>Hebrew, Greek, and Latin</note> and those of several other nations) into the Nether-Dutch <note>Dutch</note> speech and rhymes, very concisely and learnedly. And as he was now ignited with singular love and study toward the Holy Scripture, he approached that great and godly work, namely the Bible. And just as *Luther* translated the same from the Hebrew into High-German, and the French into their tongue; so has he also translated it from the same holy language into the Netherlandish speech, with great diligence and industry, by the desire and promotion of the Honorable and Mighty Lords of the States of this Land: writing something now and then in his manner which he intended for the Church and the Father-

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Philips van Marnix, Lord of Saint-Aldegonde, focusing on his later literary contributions, his diplomatic service, and his final years in Leiden dedicated to biblical translation.</meta>

# MARNIX.

...considered to serve the cause of the Netherlands: among which is a Treatise published against the heresy of the "Free Spirits" <note>The "Vry-geesten" or Libertines were a radical sect that Marnix vehemently opposed</note>, specifically those who, by overturning the Holy Scripture as God's word, base almost the entire foundation or the certainty of the same upon their completely different-sounding allegories, strange interpretations, and spiritual imaginings, which draw out and produce godless and cruel errors. Finally, he was sent as an Ambassador to France, from where he returned with poor health, being greatly broken and weakened by the hardships of that journey; nevertheless, he spent the remainder of his life in care and labor: advising and exhorting now these, then those Lords and Princes. He passed away at *Leiden* (in which city he lived for some years as if in a harbor from his labors, spending his time on the excellent Translation of the *Bibles* <note>Marnix was commissioned by the States-General to produce a new Dutch translation of the Bible from the original languages</note>) in the year 1598, after he had lived 60 years. Upon the deeds of this most excellent Hero, this verse, containing within itself an excellent praise, was published, written by a very renowned and learned man to whom the learning and studies of *Aldegonde* were well known.

->PHILIP MARNIX, LORD<-
->OF SAINT-ALDEGONDE.<-

How great a love for the Fatherland and God's word
The Lord of Saint-Aldegonde bore, his care and labor teaches:
He whom the Pope and the Spaniard—nor does the heart thank either—
Both the Fatherland and the Truth mourn his death.

This is the well-known Saint-Aldegondius, clear;
Who made the Pope's confused deceit public:
Spoke well before King and Prince, the Spaniard's heavy enemy:
A great friend was he of the Great Fatherland's Father <note>A reference to William of Orange, "Vader des Vaderlands"</note>.

# PHILIP

Alcides <note>A name for Hercules, the Greek hero</note> was once known to the world as the Tamer of Monsters,
Nestor was wise and sweet-spoken in his counsel.
Codrus <note>A legendary King of Athens who sacrificed himself for his city</note> was not afraid to pour out his life for his fatherland,
And Aristotle was known as learned in every way.
SAINT-ALDEGONDE alone was all these things to the Belgians <note>original: "Belgis" - referring here to the people of the Low Countries/Netherlands</note>,
An Alcides, a Nestor, a Codrus, and an Aristotle.

->That is:<-

Alcides, Tamer of Monsters, was once widely renowned,
And Nestor, sweet-tongued, gave very good counsel.
Codrus gave up his life unto death for the Fatherland,
And Aristotle’s learning is living still.
The Netherlands found all these together in SAINT-ALDEGONDE alone:
A pure Alcides, Nestor, Codrus, and Aristotle.

---

In this tomb is buried that famous PHILIP MARNIX,
Renowned for his counsel and powerful in his eloquence.
He who was the heart and soul of that Great Prince of Orange,
To whom the Belgian <note>The inhabitant of the Netherlands</note> owes his safety.
The free Republic felt his great assistance,
As did the honor of the pure Religion <note>Referring to the Reformed/Protestant faith</note> which he defended.
Through the thick arrows of envy and a thousand labors,
He truly climbed to the stars with a Herculean step.

->That is:<-

Philip Marnix, whom this grave holds here,
Was select in counsel and possessed great eloquence.
He who was called the heart and spirit of the Great Prince of Orange
(From whom the Netherlands holds its welfare).
The Commonwealth felt his great help in her Freedom,
As did the honor of the upright Religion.
There he withstood the shafts of hate and a thousand kinds of labor;
Like Hercules, he climbs high as a very bright star.

# MARNIX.

-> He died in the Year of our Lord **1598.** <-
-> On the **15th** day of December. <-
-> AT LEIDEN. <-
<note>Original: "Lugduni Batavorum," the Latin name for Leiden, home to the university where Marnix spent his final years.</note>

Your bones could not rest more worthily elsewhere,
MARNIX, renowned alike in war and in peace,
Than in LEIDEN; which, through the arts of Mars and Pallas,
Nurturingly raises its head high above all other cities.
<note>Mars represents warfare/military skill, and Pallas Athena represents wisdom and the arts; Marnix was famous as both a soldier and a scholar.</note>

-> That is: <-
-> *Died in the Year of our Lord* **1598.** <-
-> *the 15th of December.* <-
-> AT LEIDEN. <-

No worthier place could your remains find rest,
MARNIX, great in times of war, and also in peace,
Than at LEIDEN; where the fellowship of Mars and Pallas
Exalt their Arts more than in any other City.

---

## THESE WRITINGS
### by the Lord of Saint-Aldegonde
### brought to light:

THE Psalms translated from the Hebrew into the Dutch tongue: and thereafter set into Dutch rhymes by the same.
<note>Marnix’s metrical translation of the Psalms was a landmark in Dutch Reformed worship.</note>

Theses on the certain Rule of the Church and Church traditions.
<term>Theses: formal propositions for academic or theological debate.</term>

Also on the Sacrament of the Lord’s Supper.

Answer to the Apology of Michael de Bay, Royal Professor at Leuven.
<note>Michael de Bay (Michael Baius) was a conservative Catholic theologian whose views on grace sparked significant controversy.</note>

<column-break/>

A Letter of Consolation to the brothers, Exiles, Brabanters, Flemings, people of Hainaut, Artois, and other Netherlanders scattered in foreign lands for the sake of the pure teaching of the Gospel.
<note>Refers to the "Stranger Churches" formed by refugees fleeing religious persecution during the Dutch Revolt.</note>

A Treatise on the Lord’s Supper to the King of France’s sister, married to the Duke of Lorraine.
<note>Catherine de Bourbon, a staunch Protestant despite her brother King Henry IV's conversion to Catholicism.</note>

Against the Libertines or Free-spirits.
<term>Libertines: a term used by Reformers for those who believed spiritual grace exempted them from moral law.</term>

Apologetic answer against an anonymous Libertine.

The Roman Beehive, attributed to Francisco Sonnius, first Bi-

# PHILIP
<note>Latin: Philippus</note>

Bishop of 's-Hertogenbosch, the greatest monster of the Spanish *Inquisition* <note>The ecclesiastical tribunal established to suppress heresy</note>, and certainly the chief champion and powerful Father of the new Bishops and the servants of Spanish Tyranny. And this book was printed in Dutch in the year 1571. At which time the Duke of *Alba* <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, known for his harsh rule in the Netherlands</note> practiced his cruelty most of all in the middle of the country. This Roman Beehive <note>Refers to "The Beehive of the Romish Church," a famous satire by Marnix</note> is wonderfully crafted, owing to the diverse histories gathered therein, as well as the sharp-witted proverbs, jests, and ridiculous arguments regarding the Papist theatrical fables, legends, and other trifles spread by them; so much so that the readers of the time took singular joy in it, and for many, it was a reason to pay more earnest attention to the true Christian Religion and to better bethink themselves. Which book, being accepted with the approval of the community and read by many not without fruit, seems to have furthered the Dutch cause—especially in that time—much more than some learned commentaries on Religion.

A Table, through which the Christian and Papist Religion is specifically explained. In this are

<column-break/>

many things derived from the aforementioned Beehive: and many strong arguments introduced against the Popish [system]. A text desired and read with great diligence by the Dutch and the French, printed in the year 1599.

### POLITICS, OR
### writings concerning the
### Government.

*An Oration for the Illustrious Archduke of Austria, Matthias, and the States of the Netherlands, delivered at the Imperial Diet at Worms in the month of May, 1580. Which were since set into Dutch rhymes by Baptista Houwaert, a Dutch Poet, out of love for the Fatherland.*

To this are still to be added various writings which were brought to light by him at different times. Such as Admonitions, Declarations, Treatises, Counsels, Disputations, Consolations, Explanations, and among the serious ones, also some pleasant humorous ones, and more other anonymous writings held by the same *Aldegonde*, which he—according to the request and occasion of the time—deemed useful for the wise men among his countrymen, and for the champions of the Freedom of the Fatherland and Religion.

# MARNIX.

It is also believed that the *Song* <note>The "Wilhelmus," which eventually became the national anthem of the Netherlands.</note> formerly sung in honor of Prince *William of Nassau* was composed and published by this man, at the time when the *Duke of Alba* was oppressing the Netherlands. This *Song* is composed in such a manner, and adorned and tempered with such pleasing rhymes, that it greatly stirred the hearts and minds of the common people toward a love for the Prince and the freedom of the Fatherland. Therein

<column-break/>

*Saint-ALDEGONDE* showed himself to be like another *Tyrtaeus* <note>A Greek rebel poet known for inspiring soldiers with his verses.</note>, so often praised by *Plato*. For since the Prince’s highest praise, the awakening of virtue, the lightening of sorrows, and fortunate counsels are contained within it, it ignited the common people with zeal to stand for the freedom of the Prince and the Fatherland; so much so that one must consider nothing more fitting to have been produced in that time.

# JOHN BALE.

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'O' with intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>
Among all those who have depicted the *Antichrist* <note>In Reformation literature, the "Antichrist" was a common polemical term used to refer to the Papacy.</note> for us most vividly, JOHN BALE, PETER PAUL VERGERIUS, and MATTHIAS FLACIUS ILLYRICUS are worthily beheld: who have brought into the light not only the teachings of the foremost *Antichrist*, but also his customs, ambitions, villainies, and horrible wickednesses. These gruesome matters, gathered in great heaps from old libraries, would otherwise have remained forgotten and as if dead in book-graves, had these men not cleared away the dust and cobwebs of many ancient collections; they reveal and bring forth to us the tyrannical and godless lives of the Popes and the clergy of previous centuries. *Bale* is the first among these, an Englishman born in *Suffolk*: who by his parents—as he himself testifies, being at that time burdened with many children and also misled and deceived by false prophets—was thrust, while still a child of only twelve years, into the abyss of the Carmelites or White Friars <note>original: "vrouwen-Broers Monincken." The Carmelites were known as White Friars due to their white mantles.</note> at *Norwich* in England. Having taken up the dark theology and arts of the Papists there, as well as at *Cambridge* <note>original: "Cantelberch," likely referring to the University of Cambridge where Bale studied.</note>, and the light of the Gospel having already risen, he was quite earnestly awakened by the illustrious Lord *Wentworth* <note>Thomas Wentworth, 1st Baron Wentworth, an early patron of Bale.</note>. Shortly thereafter, by the grace of God, he was transferred from that barren Mountain of the Carmelites into the green and fruitful valley of the Gospel. And having moved straight from the pulpit into the courtrooms, first at *York* <note>original: "Eberwicke," an old Dutch rendering of Eboracum/York.</note> under *Lee*, and later in London under *Stokesley*, Archbishops; but by *Cromwell*, King *Henry's* Councilor, *Bale* was delivered, on account of the elegant Comedies <note>Bale wrote several "miracle plays" and dramas to promote Protestant ideas.</note> published by him. Having become an exile through the tyranny of the Bishops, he lived for eight years in the Netherlands: where he wrote many small Treatises in English. He was recalled by *Edward* VI, an exceedingly good King, and made Bishop of *Ossory* in Ireland, where he preached Christ crucified. That godly King having been taken away, the *Marian* storm <note>The reign of Queen Mary I (Bloody Mary), during which many Protestants were persecuted.</note> drove *Bale* out of Ireland: who, forced through very many dangers, was captured by pirates, plundered, mocked, and treated inhumanely, and finally sold, and his ransom

<summary>This page provides a biographical sketch of John Bale, a former Carmelite friar turned Protestant reformer and playwright, detailing his early life, his conversion, and his persecution under Catholic authorities.</summary>
<keywords>John Bale, Reformation, Carmelites, Norwich, Thomas Wentworth, Thomas Cromwell, Henry VIII, Edward VI, Bishop of Ossory, Marian Persecution, anti-Catholicism</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of John Bale, focusing on his exile in Basel and his scholarly contributions to British literary history.</meta>

->J O H N<-

paid, released, he betook himself to Germany, as if into a secure haven of Christian Piety. Now residing in Basel, he wrote that renowned book, namely, the *Catalogue* <note>original: *Catalogum*; referring to Bale's *Scriptorum Illustrium Maioris Britanniae... Catalogus*</note> of the writers of the great and famous island of *Britain*, now called England and Scotland. Containing fourteen Centuries, or as many hundred lives and writings of learned men, which he brought to light through the printer *Oporinus* <note>Johannes Oporinus, a famous Swiss printer known for publishing Protestant works</note>. From this it appears that he examined many libraries, and rescued the books from moths and worm-holes, seeking out and gathering the names of those in *Britain* who, through almost all ages until the year of Christ 1557, have been renowned for their learning and writings. In which work he expressed with great diligence the persecutions of the Roman Popes, their lives and outcomes, and dragged the same Popes' wicked deeds out of the darkness: which evil acts, without him, would never have been so well known to the enemies of the *Antichrist*. He helped himself much with the writings and the assistance of *John Leland* of London: who was entirely inclined and devoted to *British* antiquity, as well as the name and honor of his Fatherland, which author became very famous through *Bale*. Living in Germany, he had as special friends *Alexander Alesius* and *John Knox*, Scots, to whom he dedicated part of the *Catalogue* of famous men in Scotland: just as he also dedicated a portion of his writings to *Gesner*, *Simler*, and *Lycosthenes*: which men previously enlarged and adorned their Libraries <note>referring to universal bibliographies</note> at Zurich (since printed multiple times) by means of the *Catalogue* of British learned men published by *Bale*. To whom that very diligent and polymathic *Gesner*, as a sign of special friendship, dedicated his *Mithridates*—or the distinction of Languages, both of the ancients and those which are in use today by manifold nations throughout the whole world—with annotations. What a remarkable enemy of the Roman *Antichrist* *Bale* was, one may see from *Lawrence Humphrey*, a sharp-minded investigator of Roman and Papist antiquities, who thus writes of *Bale* in his prophecy concerning the Babylonian Rome, among other things:

# BALEUS.

*Luther revealed many things, as did Platina:*
*Vergerius some, but Baleus holds them all.*
<note>Original Latin: "Plurima Luterus patefecit, Platina multa: Quædam Vergerius, cuncta Baleus habet." This couplet emphasizes that while others started the work of exposing church secrets, John Bale completed the task.</note>

->That is:<-

*Much was uncovered by Luther, and so too by Platina:*
*Likewise by Vergerius: But Baleus leaves nothing behind.*

Therefore, *Baleus* <note>John Bale (1495–1563), an English controversialist and historian</note> is the one who has dragged out for us that Cacus <note>A fire-breathing giant from Roman mythology who lived in a cave; here used as a metaphor for the Pope or the Roman Church</note> from that most foul cavern of blasphemous thievery, roguery, and the shameful and horrific-to-name unchastity, and brought him into the light of day.

# PETER PAUL
## VERGERIUS.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'N' featuring foliate and floral motifs.</image-desc>

Following JOHN BALE <note>original: "IOANNEM BALEVM"; an English historian and reformer</note> here follows PETER PAUL VERGERIUS, commonly called the Younger, Bishop of Capodistria <note>original: "Iuſtinopolen"; the Latin name for the city in Istria</note>. For there was another of the same name, who was also born in Capodistria in Istria or Slavonia, who was a disciple of *Chrysoloras* <note>Manuel Chrysoloras (c. 1355–1415), a pioneer in spreading Greek literature in Western Europe</note>, a philosopher from Constantinople teaching the Greek language, and who was a man very renowned for his writings. In this, our *Vergerius*, beloved observer, note that which is worthy of all wonder. This is a very learned man, who advanced greatly not only in Papal Theology, but also in the written Laws (so that he obtained a title therein). He was first Bishop of Modruš <note>original: "Madruſen"</note>, and thereafter chosen as Bishop of Capodistria. In the year 1530, when that very famous Assembly <note>The Diet of Augsburg</note> was held at Augsburg, he was sent into Germany as a Legate by Pope *Clement* VII. In which assembly, the religion of the Protestants (the Emperor being present there) had to be very earnestly examined. At which time, truly, the Pope was very fearful for his own sake and for the sake of the Roman state. In this difficult and most pressing time, *Peter Paul Vergerius* was esteemed as the most worthy Legate of the Pope, whom he sent with great authority to King *Ferdinand*, brother of the Emperor: but above all, as the historian *Sleidanus* <note>Johannes Sleidanus, the chronicler of the Reformation</note> relates, he charged him to apply all his diligence: *That no Council should be held in the German lands: And that Ferdinand should prevent everything that strove toward that end.* According to this desire of the Pope, *Vergerius* drove all matters forward. He honored *Faber*, *Eck*, *Cochlaeus*, and *Nausea* <note>Leading Catholic theologians and opponents of Luther</note> with status and benefices, so that they would show themselves to be pious and brave champions: furthermore, he (as the Pope's Legate) made *Eck* a Canon, by which Legate's presence the right of election usually remained suspended. When the Popes *Clement* and *Paul* had used *Vergerius’s* services in Germany for a long time, he was called back to Rome in the year [15]41, at which time they were busy in Rome choosing new Cardinals; he, *Vergerius*, among these purple-clad...

# PETER PAUL VERGERIUS

(as one who had steadily promoted the Pope's affairs) hoping also to be counted <note>likely referring to being named a Cardinal</note>. But understanding from many that hope was quite withdrawn from him, as they—due to such a long residence among the Germans—considered him essentially a Lutheran <note>original: "Lutriaen," a derogatory term for a follower of Martin Luther</note>. Here *Vergerius* stood as if stunned, and departing to his own country, he wrote, for his defense, a book which he named, *Against the Apostates of Germany*. But as he now set about refuting the writings of his adversaries and began to examine them diligently, he found himself overcome. Thereupon he departed to his own brother, *John Baptist* <note>original: "Ioannem Baptistam"</note>, Bishop of the city of Pola; declaring that he had changed from a Roman Catholic into a true Christian. The brother, at first struck dumb by this, greatly lamented his brother's condition. *Vergerius*, bringing up what was right concerning Justification <note><term>Justification: "Rechtveerdichmakinghe"</term>—The theological doctrine that believers are made righteous before God through faith alone, a central pillar of the Reformation.</note> (as a chief point of Christians), overcame his brother in that error through the clear evidence of Holy Scripture. The brother acknowledged his error and deemed the religion of the Papists to be unrighteous. These brothers, both Bishops, now embracing one another in that unity and proclaiming the benefits of Christ (which is the office of a true Christian Bishop), began to call the people to the pure doctrine of the Gospel through *Istria* or Slavonia. But the chief *Inquisitors*, *Annibal Grisonius* and *Hieronymus Mutius* (who afterwards wrote an *Invective* or vehement oration against *Vergerius*), forbade that. *Annibal Grisonius*, betaking himself in haste to Capodistria <note>original: "Iustinopolen"</note>, fell upon the citizens' houses and searched there for forbidden books. Furthermore, he also excommunicated those who did not report those among them who were notorious or suspected of the Lutheran doctrine. *Vergerius*, meanwhile hiding in Padua, frequently visited and comforted *Francis Spira* <note><note>Francis Spira was a famous case of a lawyer who recanted his Protestant faith under pressure and subsequently died in a state of profound spiritual despair.</note></note>. Moved by the sight of his miserable condition and example, he was strengthened and confirmed more from day to day in the true religion, so that leaving Italy, he arrived in the neighboring Rhaetia or the land of the Three Leagues <note>original: "Dry-bondten landt," referring to the Graubünden region of Switzerland</note>, and settled in the *Valtellina* <note>original: "Val-Telin"</note>. This valley is the loveliest and most fruitful of the whole Rhaetian land; and as it borders the Venetians and Milanese, it often happens that from all quarters of Italy, those who [acknowledge] Christ as our only Savior open-

# VERGERIUS.

openly desired to declare and confess. Therefore, in this part of **Rhaetia** <note>original: "Rhetiæ"; a Roman province in the Alps, now part of Switzerland and Italy</note> many exceptionally learned, godly, and eloquent men from Italy are commonly found, who pasture and feed Christ's sheep through God's Word in this green and lovely Valley with great fruitfulness. From which Valley **Vergerius** (after he had taught and preached the Gospel there for some years) was called to Tübingen by **Christopher**, Prince of Württemberg. The brother of this **Vergerius**, Bishop of the city of Pola, had passed away before he departed from Italy, not without suspicion of poisoning. How boldly and undauntedly **Vergerius** approached the Pope is clearly shown to us by his writings. He died at Tübingen in the year 1565.

# PIETRO PAOLO VERGE-
## R I O,  B I S S H O P.

**T**his is Vergerio, sent there from Rome,
Who among the Germans praised the Pope as very renowned;
Thereafter he praised Luther and found God's servants
Upright, but declared the Pope to be the Antichrist.

He who was formerly Bishop of Justinopolis <note>Modern-day Koper, Slovenia.</note>, became afterward
Praised as a faithful lover of the Holy God:
Whom he once praised as holy—yea, almost like Christ—
He later said was the Antichrist, entirely evil in being.

# MATTHIAS FLAC-
## CIUS ILLYRICUS.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'V' historiated with floral motifs, scrolls, and strapwork.</image-desc>

VERGERIUS from ISTRIA follows here in due order. FLACIUS from *ILLYRIA*, which today is called *Slavonia*, bordering on *Istria*. From which lands these two men came forth, flourished at the same time, and attacked the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Pope</note> with sufficiently similar weapons. *Matthias Flacius Illyricus* was born at *Albona* <note>Modern Labin, Croatia</note> of a noble family: who in the first years of his studies had heard *Baptista Egnatius*, a man well-renowned in his time for his fine learning, at *Venice*. In the 17th year of his age, he appeared at *Basel* to practice Theology, through the advice and urging of *Baldus Lupatinus*, Provincial of the *Minorites* <note>The Franciscan Order</note> at that time, and of his closest kinship. This *Baldus* (because he had accepted the *Gospel*, which they call the *Lutheran Heresy*) was cast into prison: in which, after he was entirely undone by twenty years of hardship, misery, and the filth of the stench, he was afterwards drowned in the *Sea* at *Venice*. It is a custom of the *Venetians* not to watch or examine Christians so closely and harshly: but upon those discovered, to use a new and entirely severe punishment: which is either to thrust them into the deepest and most terrible prisons, where they pine away through length of time until they die: or while living, they seem to be dead: or secretly and without anyone's knowledge, they have them put to death by the Executioner. Whereby, according to human insight and understanding, they believe they are doing their *Republic* a very useful service. When this *Illyricus* still lived at *Basel*, he had as a landlord and Master the very learned and good man *Grynaeus* <note>Simon Grynaeus, a noted Protestant reformer and humanist</note>. And he was further trained in other *Universities*, especially in those of *Tübingen* and *Wittenberg*. In the University of *Jena* he was a Professor, first in the *Hebrew* language, and thereafter in *Holy Theology*. He became famous through the writing of many of his fine books, concerning both the godless teachings and the evil life of the *Antichrist*. He discovered very many hidden things from the *Libraries* searched by him, and

# MATTHIAS FLACIUS.

and the writings of other authors brought to light, which cause us to behold—not without amazement—the deceptions, customs, earnestness, tyranny, and villainous deeds of the King of the Kingdom of Darkness. These things might have remained hidden to our detriment, and stayed as if buried in their own darkness, had he not wandered through the hidden places or chambers of the libraries. What a brave and steadfast enemy of the Roman Antichrist he was is taught by those times in which the *Interimistic* <note>original: "Interimitifche," referring to the Augsburg Interim of 1548, a temporary decree intended to reintegrate Protestants into the Catholic Church</note> or *in-between-standing Helena* <note>A metaphorical reference to Helen of Troy, suggesting the Interim was a seductive but destructive deception</note>—decorated and adorned very finely with cunning deceit and Roman rouge—was presented to the Germans. Many (mostly through the Emperor's proposal and insistence) embraced this decorated *Helena*; many also (among whom *Flacius* was one of the first) rejected all those loose harlot’s tricks and enticements with which the Pope and the Emperor endeavored to draw in and capture the German men. While living in Magdeburg (which city at that time was a refuge for German *Freedom* and the *Christian Religion*), he opposed those of Wittenberg and Leipzig, so that he might rebuke those "indifferent matters" <note>The "Adiaphora," or non-essential church practices</note> (which prepared the way for the reintroduction of the Papal Religion). Because of this, as a great enemy of the Pope, he had to endure great conflict from the *Adiaphorists* <note>term: Adiaphorists (Protestants who believed certain Catholic rites were indifferent and could be accepted for the sake of peace)</note>. He obtained a renowned name through his *Catalog of the Witnesses of the Truth* <note>original: "Register der Getuyghen der Waerheyt"</note> and his *Ecclesiastical History*, through which he greatly frustrated and weakened the *Antiquity* or *Ancientness* of the Papists (of which they boast highly). In the *Catalog* are included many men of all states and conditions, both Political and Ecclesiastical, who in past ages despised and rejected the Papacy. The *Ecclesiastical History* <note>note: Known as the "Magdeburg Centuries," the first great Protestant history of the Christian Church</note>, divided by hundreds of years [centuries], produced and printed with great labor and expense, sets before our eyes very clearly the state of the former Church: in which are above all discovered the Ambitions, Greed, Ungodliness, Villainies, Cunning, and Deceit of the Popes. That is a wonderful and great work, of which he is the first and foremost author; but his associates are *Johannes Wigandus*, *Mattheus Judex*, and *Basilius Faber*. Which [work] the Babylonian Roman builders have endeavored to break and suppress with manifold means and instruments, and that at times through those things (as is wont to happen in great works) which were not exactly according to the plumb line of judgment therein...

*Ecclesiastical History, compiled and gathered together with Wigand and Basilius Faber, organized by centuries, and completed up to the thirteenth century during his life.* <note>This refers to the "Magdeburg Centuries," the first great Protestant history of the Christian Church.</note>
*His own refutation of the Invective or vehement Oration of Brunus against the Centuries of Ecclesiastical Histories: wherein are recounted more than one hundred historical and significant lies of the Papists.*
*This wondrous, all-encompassing, and restless mind, Illyricus, has brought forth many more other books.*

# CASPAR OLEVIA-
## N U S.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap initial "W" decorated with detailed floral and leaf motifs, marking the start of the text block.</image-desc>

Who shall be able to sufficiently praise CASPAR OLEVIANUS of Trier, such a renowned Preacher of God’s Word? For while he was still a boy, he was zealous to be instructed in the knowledge of God and also to teach others therein, through a cause apparently small, but in deed great. For by chance reading (as he himself testifies in his preface to the exposition of the *Apostolic Faith* <note>The Apostles' Creed</note>, where he addresses the youth inclined toward godliness) a certain learned man’s writing, which exhorted the youth in this manner: *There is no more glorious thing, he says, than to instruct men in the knowledge of God, concerning the creation of the human race, concerning its fall into sin, and concerning the reconciliation and rebuilding through the Son of God, that promised seed of the woman which shall bruise the serpent’s head, etc.* Olevianus was a young man of fifteen years when he read this divine proverb: which, pressing it into his memory and well-chewing <note>ruminating or deeply considering</note> it, he considered as specifically prescribed for him: which little spark he since steadily fanned in his mind through the reading of the Holy Scriptures. Before his fourteenth year <note>likely a reference to his early education prior to Paris</note> he was sent to the High School <term>University: the term 'Hooghe Schole' refers to a university-level institution</term> of Paris: where through fine diligence he learned and acquired those sciences, discipline, and instruction which brought him no small assistance in that holy Office, which he afterwards served with such faithfulness and steadfastness. What an excellent and outstanding Preacher he was, many still testify to us today who heard him with exceeding great pleasure: how perfectly he cut through <note>rightly divided</note> that which was in God’s word and impressed it upon his hearers, serving the confirmation of our Christian faith, as his fine writings still clearly show us: and principally the exposition of the *Apostolic Faith*, or the articles of the Faith taken from his Sermons, which he desired as witnesses of the upright and faithful Evangelical teaching, which he had preached for fifteen years in the Heidelberg Church (laying the right foundation of the Faith). He had also previously in his...

# CASPAR

In his homeland of Trier, he opened the Fountain of the Gospel for those thirsting after Jesus Christ, pouring out the waters of Life; these waters also flowed and pressed through the fields of neighboring lands, bringing forth a joyful harvest in due season. Through great necessity and many dangers, *Olevianus* praised and broke through this spring-head (which had been clogged by the mud of human institutions <note>original: "menschelijcker insettingen," referring to man-made traditions or laws obstructing pure scripture</note>) in the year of our Lord 1560. How excellent a preacher and teacher he was is declared to us by the judgments of the learned, who, in their books containing the method or science of preaching well, call this man a very eloquent and earnest preacher: so much so that they occasionally present him as a patron or model of a good preacher to godly youths inclined toward that office. How dear and pleasant our subject was to *Frederick the Pious* <note>Frederick III, Elector Palatine (1515–1576)</note> or the Godly Count Palatine, is shown by the aforementioned exposition of the Faith, dedicated by him to the Prince in the year 1576, shortly before this highly-praised Prince—a true Father and Foster-Lord of the Churches—departed alongside those whom we believe live and reign rightly. Such a shepherd and laborer was *Olevianus*, as the Lord's flock and the Gospel harvest today abundantly wish for and desire.

->OLEVIANUS HAS<-
->left us these Writings:<-

Sermons on the Faith and Obedience of the Patriarch Abraham.

Sermons on the Lord's Supper.

Exhortation on the matter of the Lord's Supper.

A Table <note>A systematic chart or summary</note> of the ministry of the Word and the Sacraments.

The exposition of the Apostolic Creed or the Articles of Faith: wherein the whole concept of the eternal

<column-break/>

Covenant of Grace between God and the Believers is briefly and clearly treated.

An Epitome or short summary of the instruction in the Christian Religion by *John Calvin*, specifically for the use of the school of Herborn: which *Dr. Piscator* <note>Johannes Piscator (1546–1625), a prominent theologian</note> later, following *Olevianus* therein, arranged into systematic articles. Both of them dedicated their labor to *Dr. Beza* <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), successor to Calvin in Geneva</note>.

Annotations on the letter to the *Galatians*.

# PETER MARTYR.

Peter Martyr, a Florentine, descended from *Stefano Vermilio* and *Maria Fumantina*, of an ancient, renowned, noble, and honorable lineage, had the advantage from his parents of being raised from youth in all good arts and proper manners. As they had only one son and were provided with good means, they were able to carry out their good intentions in this regard. The mother, moreover, being skilled in the Latin language, explained the comedies or plays of *Terence* <note>Publius Terentius Afer, a Roman playwright whose works were used to teach pure Latin style.</note> to her young son. This followed the example of certain excellent women among the ancient Romans, from whom—as their mothers—the *Gracchi*, *Laelii*, *Catuli*, *Julii*, and other select orators received and brought to light the purity and lovely pronunciation of the Latin tongue. Just as in our time, *Olympia Morata* (an Italian), *Jane Suffolk* <note>Lady Jane Grey, known for her profound learning and brief reign as Queen of England.</note>, and *Lady Bacon* <note>Ann Cooke Bacon, mother of Francis Bacon and a brilliant scholar.</note> (Englishwomen) have been very renowned in learning and eloquence. These excellent and princely women, through a manly spirit and earnestness, opposed the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Papacy.</note>. Especially the most outstanding *Lady Bacon*, adorned with true virtues and the marks of her nobility, who very gracefully translated from Latin into the English tongue the *Apology* or Defense of the English Church, written by *Jewel*, Bishop of *Salisbury*. This *Apology* was judged by *Bullinger*, *Gualther*, *Wolff*, as well as our *Martyr*, to be as well-crafted and perfect as anything printed in those times.

During those years in which—according to the Italian nature and that of his homeland—all lewdness abounded, he rejected unlawful pleasure and entered the path of virtue. He committed himself to the monastic life (which at that time alone was considered holy) and was consequently accepted into the Foundation of the Canons Regular of Saint *Augustine* (who, for the sake of their learning and discipline, were well-regarded by good people throughout *Italy* at that time). The father little approved of this deed of his son, as one who had undoubtedly often heard the rebukes and sermons of *Girolamo Savonarola* <note>A Dominican friar and preacher in Florence known for his prophecies and calls for Christian renewal.</note> directed at the monks and the Roman clergy.

He went to Padua for his studies, and in Philosophy,

# PETER

Being well-versed in Scholastic Theology <note>A medieval method of learning that emphasizes dialectical reasoning</note>, and the Greek and Hebrew languages, he began to preach as a Regular Monk <note>A monk living under a specific religious rule, likely the Augustinians</note> at the age of twenty-six; first at *Brixen*, and thereafter in the renowned cities of Italy, namely *Rome*, *Bologna*, *Pisa*, *Venice*, *Mantua*, and *Bergamo*. In the College of his residence, he interpreted Philosophy and the Holy Scripture, as well as *Homer* elsewhere. Through his learning and great diligence, he was chosen Abbot of *Spoleto*, and thereafter Provost of the Neapolitan College, namely *St. Peter ad Aram* <note>original: "St. Peeters ad Aram"</note>. Three years later, he was declared General, as they call it, of the Order: finally, he was appointed Prior of *St. Frediano* at *Lucca*. In which city *Martyr*, to the best of his ability, promoted Learning: because of this, even today the *Lucchese* feel the fruits of the same *Martyr's* labor; therefore, the Evangelical doctrine is not so entirely obscure or unknown among them. And as some treacherous snares were finally laid for *Martyr*, he left the city because of them and went to *Zurich*, where, according to the good nature of those people, he was received and friendly entertained by *Bullinger*, *Pellican*, *Gualter*, and more other servants of those Churches and Schools. Shortly thereafter, *Martyr* was called to *Strasbourg* through the persistence of *Bucer*: and after he had taught the Holy Scripture in the same city for five years, he traveled to *England* at the request of *Thomas Cranmer*, and at the desire of the most Godly King <note>Edward VI</note>, and was appointed to the interpretation of the Holy Scripture at *Oxford* by His Majesty's command. In which High School he first interpreted the first epistle of *Paul* to the *Corinthians*. Through the teaching of which epistle, he endeavored to remove and amend the faults by which the integrity of the Church was corrupted, as also to ward off all abuses and Papist institutions therefrom. A heavy war regarding the Supper of the Lord <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> *Martyr* had to engage in here with the Roman *Cyclopes* <note>A derogatory term used here for fierce Catholic opponents</note>, who, to maintain and defend their God-and-man-devouring <note>A polemical reference to the doctrine of Transubstantiation</note>, were raging sufficiently in their cruelty. The King now being deceased, the *Marian* Storm and Tempest <note>The reign of Queen Mary I and the subsequent persecution of Protestants</note> also drove *Martyr* away. Having departed from England and returned again to *Strasbourg*, he obtained his former place. Where, on the matter of the Lord's Supper, a certain discord arose, in which he experienced great opposition. Being called to *Zurich*, he went from there with *John Jewel*, an Englishman, a Theologian most eminent in spirit and learning. Since then, by the command and at the desire of the honorable Council of the City, he [attended] the assembly or Colloquy of *Poissy* <note>original: "Poẞi"</note>

<meta>catchword: ghe- (likely "gheweest" - has been)</meta>

It highlights his academic contributions at Oxford and his eventual flight from England during the reign of Queen Mary I.</summary>

D. LXII.</note> on the <term>11th</term> <note>original: XI.</note> of November, after he had lived <term>63</term> <note>original: LXIII.</note> years. What kind of man and how great a man <term>Martyr</term> <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), a significant Italian-born Reformed theologian</note> was, is shown clearly enough to us by the Memoirs and Books he left behind for Christendom.

# HIERONYMUS ZANCHIUS.

->Hieronymus ZANCHIUS of BERGAMO<- <note>original: "Bergomo"</note> ought to be added here alongside Martyr the Florentine <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), a leading Reformed theologian</note>: with good reason, the *Prior of Lucca* <note>original: "Prior de Soos," referring to the San Frediano monastery in Lucca</note> follows him. Blessed are the companions of that assembly who knew and loved *Martyr* the *Prior* as a *Father* <note>original: "Pater"</note> or true father. Therefore, we may here apply that old proverb, *Like Abbot, Like Monk*, in a positive sense. For this *Martyr*, through his example and his preceding faithful teaching and good morals, so strengthened and advanced the flock that he brought many from that Order or society (of which *Martyr* was the *Provost* or head) to the blessed nourishment of the Gospel. We can easily grasp this from the fact that since *Martyr’s* departure from *Italy*, within the space of one year, eighteen companions left that assembly; stripping themselves of the surcoats of the *Antichristians* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Roman Catholic Church hierarchy</note>, they joined those Churches in which no idols, but the living Word of the Gospel, held place. Among these were *Paulus Lacisius*, first a *Professor* of the *Latin* language in *Italy* and later of the *Greek* language at *Strasbourg*; the very noble man *Celsus Martinengbus* <note>Celsus Martinengo</note>, descended from the illustrious house of the Counts of *Martinengo*, who taught the *Greek* language in that *College* or assembly and led the *Italian* Church in *Geneva* with great praise; likewise *Emanuel Tremellius* <note>original: "Trmellius"</note>, that very famous interpreter of the *Hebrew* tongue; and here our *Hieronymus Zanchius*, a man born of noble lineage, who explained the Holy Scriptures at *Strasbourg* with his friend *Martyr*. He was a man highly experienced in the Church Fathers and *Philosophy*: who, as he demonstrated the doctrine of the *Ubiquity* <note><term>Ubiquity: the Lutheran doctrine that Christ's body is present everywhere at once, which Zanchius and other Reformed theologians rejected</term></note> of Christ’s Body—or its fleshly presence everywhere—to be false, could in no way tolerate it; thus it happened that many (as also previously with *Martyr*) would not suffer him there because of it. So it was that the same cause which moved *Martyr* to leave *Strasbourg* also moved his companion *Zanchius*: who thereafter embraced the *Heidelberg Academy* or high school, being favored by the godly Prince *Frederick* <note>Frederick III, Elector Palatine</note> and

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>This page details the life of Hieronymus Zanchius and his departure from Italy alongside other scholars influenced by Peter Martyr Vermigli. It highlights the mass conversion of the Lucca monastics to Protestantism and their subsequent academic careers in Strasbourg, Geneva, and Heidelberg.</summary>
<keywords>Hieronymus Zanchius, Peter Martyr Vermigli, Lucca, Strasbourg, Heidelberg, Protestant Reformation, Ubiquity, Emanuel Tremellius, Italian Reformers</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the early life or academic career of Hieronymus Zanchius before his departure from his previous post due to theological disputes.</meta>

# HIERONYMUS
## 118

held in honor. When Frederick, named the Pious <note>Frederick III, Elector Palatine (1515–1576), a major supporter of Reformed theology</note>, had died, and the dispute over the aforementioned *Ubiquity* <note>The Lutheran doctrine that Christ's body is present everywhere; a major point of contention with Reformed theologians</note> began to rule and prevail there, the enemies of that doctrine were forced to change their location. Among these was *Zanchius*: who thereafter served as Pastor at Chiavenna <note>original: "Clavenne"</note>, which is a famous city in *Rhaetia* or the land of the Three Leagues <note>Now part of modern-day Switzerland</note>, situated not far from the Lake of Como. There, merchants from the Duchy of Milan and Italy conveniently transport their wares and offer them for sale at Chiavenna. Because this city is closest to Italy and situated in a convenient place (as are the other fertile valleys nearby), many pious Italian men who have cast off the yoke of the *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Papacy</note> live there. Finally, at Neustadt, a city in the Palatinate, under the leadership of the Pious Prince *Casimir* <note>John Casimir of the Palatinate (1543–1592)</note>, he interpreted the Holy Scriptures; until, through the passing of years and the infirmity of his old age, he was granted by the aforementioned Prince the right to live out his life in rest. 

He was a man (as appears from his writings) of singular moderation and skill, and a great promoter of the church's rest and peace; whose quietness and moderation, however, counted for little among those who so immoderately loved and advocated for *Ubiquity*. He stood by his friend *Sturmius* <note>Johannes Sturmius (1507–1589), a significant educator and diplomat</note> in this strife or discord, as they both advocated for and defended the true *Confession* or Profession of Augsburg, and strongly opposed *Ubiquity*. With this *Sturmius* (a pious and grave man), *Zanchius* maintained a great friendship throughout his life. Having now grown old, he addressed and exhorted the aged *Sturmius* (as they both narrowed the boundaries of their labors) with lovely words, and among others used these (from which the earnestness and striving of both is evident): *Truly, Master Sturmius, if there ever was a time, it is now more than ever upon us both, that we turn and lift the eyes of our minds and our hearts from the world to Christ: and in Heaven above all stars, where Christ dwells in the body, frequently behold Him with the godly spirits: knowing and most firmly hoping, that we expect to live there very soon with Christ and His holy Angels.* Which, according to the feeling of his soul, happened to *Zanchius*, where he passed away in Heidelberg in the year of Christ's birth 1590, in November, being about 76 years after his birth, following his friend *Sturmius*, who was 80 years old.

<summary>This page details the later career of the theologian Hieronymus Zanchius, including his exile due to the Ubiquity controversy, his ministry in Chiavenna and Neustadt, and his friendship with Johannes Sturmius before his death in 1590.</summary>

# BY ZANCHIUS WERE
### these books in print
### issued:

*Theological Miscellanies, in which various controversies are very strongly handled, and also the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, which Zanchius here interprets from a Reformed perspective</note> is explained.*

*Judgment on the controversy regarding the matter of the Lord's Supper.*

*XIII. Books on the Trinity: the Eternal Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, one and the same God, divided into two parts: Wherein the first part explains and confirms the entirely orthodox <note>original: "Gheloof-matich," meaning consistent with the creeds</note> doctrine of this great mystery, drawn from the fountain of Holy Scripture: And in the latter part are refuted all the fabrications and sophistries driven against this teaching by the Antichristian plagues.*

*Answer to a certain Arian booklet, which is named, "Opposition of the teaching of Christ and Antichrist, concerning one true God."*

*Answer to a part of the "Forerunner" by Wilhelm Holder: in which there is a pleasant dispute concerning the visions in which, after the Ascension, Christ showed himself in his true body to both Stephen and Paul.*

*A Compendium of the principal head-articles of the Christian Religion.*

*A whole Treatise on the Holy Scripture: Containing all the controversies of that argument, drawn from the fountain of Holy Scripture, as well as from the orthodox and venerable writings of the Church Fathers, expressing itself clearly through a certain order.*

*Prefaces printed on the Physics, with Aristotle's Greek books, on Natural Philosophy <note>original: "Natuerlycke Tochooringhe," referring to Aristotle's 'Physica' or 'Natural Hearing'</note>.*

-># MARTIN CHEMNITZ<-
<note>original: "Martinus Kemnitius"</note>

What remarkable and fierce enemies of the **Antichrist** the German **Martins**—namely **Luther**, **Bucer**, and this **Chemnitz**—have been, they have clearly enough proven through their **studies** and **struggles**. And just as those first two primarily stormed **Rome**, so this man has stormed **Trent** (which was occupied and defended by the Roman garrison, being filled with the Pope's spirit). Since he had followed the warfare and seen the battle-order of those Christian Doctors as his leaders—namely **Luther** and **Melanchthon**—from his youth as a young soldier; so it happened that he, finally well-instructed and experienced in the conduct and weapons of that war, fought lawfully against that triple-crowned Roman Head <note>The Pope, referring to the papal tiara</note> and his purple-clad and mitered proud heap of henchmen. And as he was very well raised and instructed from his youth in good discipline, as well as in **philosophy** and the **mathematical** arts, he prepared for himself a more suitable path to reach weightier and greater studies. When he had followed his nephew **Georg Sabinus**, Professor of the school at Königsberg <note>original: "Conincx-Bergen"</note>, he became the librarian of the Prince of Prussia. After he had studied at Wittenberg, he was called to Brunswick, a renowned and free city in Saxony, where he served his office very diligently as a shepherd and leader of those churches. This very learned and thorough theologian has acquired a name no less famous than Trent itself through its widely renowned Papal **Council** held there. For he, according to God's Word as a plumb line <term>plumb line: a tool used to determine vertical straightness, here meaning a standard of truth</term>, and the most faithful judgments and opinions of the ancient Fathers, has diligently measured and examined the decrees of the new Fathers of Trent. This is, among others, an exquisite work, or much rather an over-rich library, which contains the refutation of the **Council of Trent**, and also at the same time the explanation and confirmation of the Evangelical doctrine: entirely useful to everyone who loves the truth, and also to those who have resolved [to oppose] the Papists, who so greatly [rely] on the **Pythagorean**... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely referring to the "Pythagorean" authority where a master's word is taken without proof.</note>
